## Classical Greek Philosophy and the Socratic Schools (c. 470–300 BCE)

### Socrates ☆ (c. 469–399 BCE)

**Position:** Classical Greek Philosophy · Socratic Philosophy · Moral Philosophy · Dialectical Inquiry

* **Name:** Socrates (Ancient Greek: Σωκράτης, *Sōkrátēs*; /ˈsɒkrətiːz/).
* **Life:** Athenian philosopher; served as a soldier during the Peloponnesian War. Tried in Athens for impiety and corrupting the young; sentenced to death and executed by drinking hemlock in 399 BCE.
* **Contributions:** A foundational figure in Western moral philosophy. Associated with systematic ethical questioning, conceptual definition, examination of beliefs, and the philosophical ideal of the examined life. His influence gave rise to several distinct Socratic schools.
* **Primary Sources:** No writings by Socrates survive. Principal accounts: Plato's *Apology*, *Euthyphro*, *Crito*, *Laches*, *Charmides*, *Meno*; Xenophon's *Memorabilia*, *Apology*, *Symposium*; Aristophanes' *Clouds*; Aristotle's discussions of Socrates.

#### Stories about Socrates

During the fifth century BCE, **Athens** became the political and intellectual centre of the Greek world. Following the Greek victories over Persia, the city established a powerful maritime empire, developed its democratic institutions, and attracted philosophers, poets, historians, and professional teachers from across the Mediterranean. Its cultural achievements reached their height under **Pericles**, but its growing rivalry with Sparta eventually led to the devastating **Peloponnesian War (431–404 BCE)**. **Socrates** was born around 469 BCE in **Alopece**, a district of Athens, and lived through the city's rise to imperial greatness, its military defeat, and the violent political struggles that followed.

Socrates was the son of **Sophroniscus**, traditionally described as a stoneworker, and **Phaenarete**, whom Plato identifies as a midwife. He received the customary education of an Athenian citizen, including literature, music, and physical training. Unlike the aristocratic youths who later surrounded him, Socrates apparently possessed only modest financial resources. He nevertheless became one of the most recognisable figures in Athens, partly because of his unconventional appearance. Ancient descriptions portray him with a broad face, protruding eyes, a flat nose, and thick lips, resembling **Silenus**, the grotesque companion of Dionysus. He frequently walked barefoot, wore a simple cloak, and displayed remarkable indifference to wealth, luxury, and physical discomfort.

Little is known with certainty about his early intellectual development. In Plato's *Phaedo*, Socrates recalls becoming fascinated by **natural philosophy**, particularly questions about the causes of generation, destruction, and celestial motion. He investigated contemporary explanations of nature and became especially interested in **Anaxagoras**, whose theory introduced *Nous* (Mind or Intellect) as the ordering principle of the universe. Socrates initially hoped that this theory would explain why things existed and were arranged as they were. He became disappointed, however, when Anaxagoras appeared to rely chiefly on material mechanisms rather than explaining why a particular arrangement was best. This intellectual autobiography is presented through Plato's literary reconstruction and cannot be dated precisely, but it illustrates Socrates' movement towards questions about explanation, reason, and the good.

Plato's *Parmenides* presents a young Socrates encountering the elderly **Parmenides** and his associate **Zeno of Elea** during their visit to Athens. Socrates proposes an early version of the theory of Forms, only to have Parmenides expose serious difficulties in his arguments. The encounter is philosophically significant, although its historical authenticity remains uncertain. Socrates also lived among the intellectual activities of the **Sophists**, including Protagoras, Gorgias, Prodicus, and Hippias, who offered instruction in rhetoric, argumentation, and civic excellence. Unlike these professional teachers, however, Socrates claimed no specialised knowledge of virtue and refused to charge fees for philosophical conversation.

Socrates was not merely a contemplative thinker. As an Athenian citizen, he served as a **hoplite**, a heavily armed infantryman, during the Peloponnesian War. Around 432 BCE, he joined the military expedition against **Potidaea**, where he served alongside the young aristocrat **Alcibiades**. In Plato's *Symposium*, Alcibiades recalls that Socrates endured freezing temperatures, hunger, and the hardships of military life with extraordinary composure. While other soldiers wrapped themselves in additional clothing, Socrates supposedly walked barefoot over the frozen ground. On another occasion, he became absorbed in a philosophical problem and remained standing in contemplation from morning until the following dawn.

At Potidaea, Socrates also distinguished himself through courage. According to Alcibiades, when the young soldier was wounded in battle, Socrates remained beside him and saved both his life and his armour. Alcibiades later insisted that Socrates deserved the military distinction awarded for the engagement. These recollections form part of Plato's dramatic portrayal, but Socrates' military service itself is well established.

Socrates subsequently fought at the **Battle of Delium (424 BCE)**, where the Athenians suffered a serious defeat. During the retreat, many soldiers fled in confusion. Alcibiades describes Socrates moving steadily through the battlefield, observing his surroundings and maintaining his composure despite the danger. His controlled behaviour supposedly discouraged enemy soldiers from attacking him. Socrates also served at **Amphipolis (422 BCE)**, another disastrous encounter for Athens.

The contrast between his physical appearance and inner character became one of the defining features of his reputation. The same man who wandered through Athens in worn clothing could withstand the hardships of battle, resist temptation, and maintain his independence among powerful political figures.

At some uncertain point in his life, Socrates developed the practice that would make him famous: **examining the beliefs of other people through sustained questioning**. He spent much of his time in markets, gymnasia, private houses, and other public spaces, conversing with politicians, craftsmen, poets, aristocrats, and young men. Rather than delivering formal lectures, he asked apparently simple questions: What is courage? What is justice? What makes a person virtuous? What does it mean to know something?

His conversations frequently followed a recognisable pattern. Someone confidently offered a definition. Socrates examined its implications, presented counterexamples, and asked further questions until the original answer appeared inconsistent or inadequate. His interlocutors often discovered that they could not explain concepts they had previously believed they understood. This method of critical examination became known as the **Socratic *elenchus***, or method of refutation.

In Plato's *Apology*, Socrates explains the origins of his philosophical mission through a famous story involving his friend **Chaerephon**. Chaerephon had visited the sanctuary of Apollo at **Delphi** and asked the oracle whether anyone was wiser than Socrates. The priestess reportedly answered that no one was wiser. Socrates found the response perplexing because he did not believe himself to possess any exceptional wisdom.

Determined to understand the oracle, he began examining people reputed to be knowledgeable. He first questioned prominent politicians and discovered that some possessed considerable public authority without being able to defend their claims to wisdom. He then approached poets, whose works he admired, but found that they often struggled to explain their own compositions. Finally, he examined craftsmen, recognising their genuine technical expertise while observing that their success in particular occupations sometimes led them to assume knowledge of unrelated matters.

Socrates eventually concluded that his superiority, if he possessed any, consisted in recognising the limitations of his knowledge. Other people believed themselves wise without sufficient justification; he at least understood that he did not know what he did not know. He interpreted the oracle as a divine instruction to continue examining those who claimed wisdom.

This activity brought him admirers but also created enemies. Prominent citizens resented being publicly embarrassed, while young observers enjoyed watching established authorities reduced to confusion. Some began imitating his questioning, provoking further hostility. Socrates nevertheless considered his activity a service to Athens, arguing that citizens should concern themselves not merely with wealth, reputation, or political power, but with **the condition of their souls and the cultivation of virtue**.

His private religious experiences contributed to his unusual reputation. Socrates repeatedly referred to a **divine sign (*daimonion*)** that had accompanied him since childhood. In Plato's account, this inner warning prevented him from undertaking certain actions but did not provide a comprehensive system of religious or moral instruction. Although Socrates practised traditional religious observances, his claims concerning divine guidance distinguished him from conventional religious authorities.

By **423 BCE**, Socrates had become sufficiently notorious to appear as the central figure in **Aristophanes' comedy *Clouds***. The play portrays him as the leader of a ridiculous intellectual institution, the *Thinkery*, where students investigate absurd natural phenomena, reject traditional gods, and learn how to make dishonest arguments appear convincing. At one point, the fictional Socrates appears suspended above the ground in a basket, supposedly contemplating the heavens. The comedy exaggerates and combines characteristics associated with natural philosophers and Sophists, and cannot be treated as a factual depiction of Socrates' school. Nevertheless, its caricature reveals how some Athenians perceived his activities: intellectually fashionable, irreverent, socially disruptive, and potentially dangerous to traditional education.

Socrates attracted a diverse circle of associates, including **Plato, Xenophon, Crito, Antisthenes, Phaedo, and Alcibiades**. These relationships were not organised into a formal school, and Socrates denied being a teacher in the conventional sense. The young men who accompanied him often came from wealthy families, but his conversations were not restricted to aristocrats.

Among his most remarkable relationships was his association with **Alcibiades**, the exceptionally handsome, ambitious, and politically influential Athenian aristocrat. In Plato's *Symposium*, set at a banquet celebrating the tragedian Agathon's victory in 416 BCE, Alcibiades arrives intoxicated and delivers an extraordinary speech about Socrates.

He compares Socrates to a carved figure of Silenus, whose unattractive exterior conceals images of gods. According to Alcibiades, Socrates' outwardly ordinary conversations conceal profound insights into virtue and human life. He also confesses that listening to Socrates produces an emotional reaction more powerful than the speeches of celebrated orators. Socrates makes him ashamed of neglecting his own character while pursuing political ambition.

Alcibiades further describes his unsuccessful attempt to seduce Socrates. Believing that his beauty might secure access to the older man's wisdom, he arranged to spend a night beside him. Socrates refused to exploit the situation, treating him with the same restraint he displayed elsewhere. The episode reverses the conventional expectation that an older admirer would pursue a beautiful young man. Here, the beautiful aristocrat desires the philosopher, whose attraction lies in his character and intellectual independence.

The *Symposium* is a literary dialogue rather than a transcript, but its portrayal of the relationship became central to Socrates' later image. Alcibiades himself would acquire a far more controversial reputation. During the Peloponnesian War, he repeatedly changed political allegiances, serving Athens, Sparta, and Persian interests at different times. His career contributed to public anxieties about the political influence of Socrates' associates.

Socrates' household life was considerably less prominent in the surviving accounts. He married **Xanthippe**, with whom he had three sons: Lamprocles, Sophroniscus, and Menexenus. Later writers portrayed Xanthippe as quarrelsome and difficult, producing numerous anecdotes about her disputes with her husband. One famous story describes her pouring water over Socrates after an argument, prompting him to observe that rain naturally follows thunder. The anecdote is probably legendary and reveals more about the later comic image of the philosopher than about their historical marriage. In Plato's *Phaedo*, Xanthippe appears in a markedly different situation, grieving with their youngest child during her husband's final imprisonment.

Despite his reputation, Socrates did not entirely withdraw from civic responsibility. In **406 BCE**, following the Athenian naval victory at **Arginusae**, several generals were accused of failing to rescue shipwrecked sailors and recover the bodies of the dead. Public anger led the Assembly to propose judging the generals collectively, contrary to established legal procedures. Socrates happened to be serving on the council's presiding committee and opposed the unlawful proceedings. According to Plato and Xenophon, he refused to cooperate despite intense pressure from the assembled citizens.

Two years later, Athens surrendered to Sparta, ending the Peloponnesian War. In **404 BCE**, an oligarchic government known as the **Thirty Tyrants** replaced the democracy and began executing political opponents. Its leading figures included **Critias**, who had previously associated with Socrates, and **Charmides**, another member of his intellectual circle.

Socrates' relationships with these men complicated his political reputation. Yet he also defied the new regime. When the Thirty ordered him and four other citizens to arrest **Leon of Salamis**, a man targeted for execution, Socrates refused to participate. He returned home rather than carry out the order, despite the danger of disobeying the authorities. He later recalled the incident as evidence that he would not commit injustice merely to preserve his life.

The Thirty were overthrown in **403 BCE**, and democracy was restored. Athens, however, remained deeply divided after years of defeat, civil conflict, executions, and political betrayal. Although a general amnesty sought to prevent renewed political persecution, memories of the oligarchic regime remained powerful. Socrates' association with controversial aristocrats, his criticism of conventional political expertise, and his reputation for unsettling young men all made him a vulnerable figure. The extent to which these political circumstances directly motivated his eventual prosecution remains disputed.

In **399 BCE**, at approximately seventy years of age, Socrates was formally accused of **impiety and corrupting the young**. The principal accuser was **Meletus**, supported by **Anytus**, a prominent democratic politician, and **Lycon**, an orator. The indictment alleged that Socrates failed to recognise the gods acknowledged by Athens, introduced new divine beings, and corrupted the city's youth.

Plato's *Euthyphro* places Socrates near the office of the king archon, the magistrate responsible for religious cases, shortly before his trial. There he encounters **Euthyphro**, a man who claims to understand religious duty and is prosecuting his own father. Socrates asks him to explain the nature of piety. Their conversation exposes difficulties in defining what makes an action holy, including whether the gods love what is holy because it is holy or whether it becomes holy because they love it. The dialogue is a philosophical composition, but it presents a characteristic image of Socrates continuing to investigate fundamental concepts even while facing prosecution.

Socrates was tried before a large citizen jury of approximately five hundred Athenians. In Plato's *Apology*, he refuses to deliver the conventional emotional appeal expected of defendants. Instead, he examines the accusations, challenges Meletus' reasoning, and explains the philosophical mission to which he has devoted his life.

He argues that someone who intentionally corrupted the people around him would expose himself to harm from those he had corrupted. If he had corrupted anyone unintentionally, he should have been corrected rather than prosecuted. He also rejects the accusation of complete atheism, observing that belief in divine signs or activities seems difficult to reconcile with a denial of all divine existence.

His defence eventually becomes a declaration of intellectual and moral independence. In Plato's presentation, Socrates insists that his obligation to examine himself and others is more important than preserving his life through submission.

He explains that if the Athenians offered to acquit him on the condition that he abandon philosophy, he would refuse. He would continue approaching his fellow citizens, questioning their priorities, and asking why they devoted so much attention to money, reputation, and honour while neglecting wisdom, truth, and the improvement of their souls. He would remind them that virtue is not valuable because it produces wealth; rather, wealth and other possessions become genuinely beneficial through virtue. He compares himself to a **gadfly** attached by the god to a large but sluggish horse: Athens may resent his persistent questioning, yet the city needs to be awakened from complacency.

Socrates also refuses to bring his children before the jury to provoke sympathy. Such behaviour, he argues, would compromise his dignity and encourage the judges to decide the case through emotion rather than justice.

The jury finds him guilty. Under Athenian legal procedure, the prosecution and defence then propose alternative penalties. Meletus demands death. Socrates provocatively suggests that his public service deserves free meals in the **Prytaneum**, an honour normally granted to distinguished citizens. He subsequently agrees to propose a monetary fine, with Plato and several friends offering financial guarantees. The jury nevertheless sentences him to death.

Following the verdict, Plato presents Socrates delivering a final address to his judges. His argument is not simply that death should be welcomed, but that moral wrongdoing is more dangerous than death itself. He suggests that a person determined to survive at any cost may escape execution only by abandoning the principles that make life worthwhile.

He also warns those who condemned him that killing one troublesome questioner will not eliminate criticism. Others, particularly younger men, will continue examining the conduct of their fellow citizens. The better response to criticism is not to silence those who question one's life, but to improve that life.

Turning to those who supported his acquittal, Socrates reflects on the possibility that death is not an evil. If death is the complete absence of consciousness, it might resemble a peaceful and dreamless sleep. If the soul instead survives and enters another world, he imagines encountering figures such as Homer, Hesiod, and the heroes of earlier generations. He could continue asking them questions, testing their claims to wisdom, and conversing without fear of execution.

At the conclusion of the *Apology*, Socrates declares that he and his judges must now depart along different paths: he to death, they to life. Which destination is better remains unknown to everyone except the divine.

These speeches are preserved through **Plato's literary reconstruction**, not a surviving court transcript. Their importance lies in the unusually detailed presentation of the moral commitments that Plato associated with his teacher.

Socrates did not immediately undergo execution. An Athenian sacred ship had departed for **Delos**, and the city prohibited executions until its return. Consequently, he remained imprisoned for several weeks.

During this interval, his wealthy friend **Crito** visited him and proposed an escape. Arrangements could be made, supporters were willing to provide money, and Socrates might continue living in another Greek city. Crito argued that allowing the execution would deprive Socrates' children of their father and expose his friends to accusations of cowardice.

In Plato's *Crito*, Socrates responds by insisting that fear, public opinion, and personal affection cannot determine what is right. The relevant question is whether escaping would constitute an injustice. He maintains that it is wrong to retaliate against wrongdoing with further wrongdoing and develops an argument concerning his obligations to the laws of Athens. Even an unjust verdict, he suggests, does not automatically justify violating the legal order under which he has lived.

The argument remains philosophically controversial, particularly because Socrates had previously refused unlawful commands from both democratic and oligarchic authorities. Nevertheless, the dialogue portrays a man unwilling to abandon his principles when they conflict with his immediate interests.

On his final day, according to Plato's *Phaedo*, Socrates received his companions in prison after his chains were removed. **Xanthippe** was present with their young child and lamented that her husband was about to speak with his friends for the last time. Socrates asked Crito to have her taken home, and the companions began their final philosophical conversation.

The discussion concerned **the nature and immortality of the soul**. Socrates argued that a philosopher should cultivate independence from bodily pleasures and prepare for death by seeking truth. His companions, especially **Simmias and Cebes**, challenged his arguments, forcing him to defend and refine them. Even in this final conversation, Socrates remained committed to examining objections rather than demanding unquestioning agreement.

When Crito asked how he wished to be buried, Socrates responded with characteristic humour. They could bury him however they pleased, provided they could catch him. He reminded his companions that the body they would bury was not identical with the person they had known.

As evening approached, Socrates bathed, said farewell to his family, and returned to his companions. The prison attendant responsible for carrying out the sentence reportedly wept as he acknowledged the prisoner's unusual gentleness and character.

Socrates then received the cup containing the poisonous preparation, traditionally identified as **hemlock**. According to Plato, he drank it calmly, without trembling or attempting to delay his death. His companions, unable to contain their grief, began crying. Socrates reproached them and asked them to maintain their composure.

As the poison took effect, his legs grew heavy, and he lay down. The attendant periodically examined his body as sensation disappeared. Shortly before his death, Socrates uncovered his face and addressed Crito with his final recorded request:

**"Crito, we owe a cock to Asclepius. Pay the debt and do not neglect it."**

**Asclepius** was the Greek god of healing, to whom grateful worshippers commonly offered sacrifices. The meaning of the request remains disputed. Some interpreters understand it as thanksgiving for release from bodily existence; others regard it as a reference to an ordinary religious obligation whose circumstances are unknown.

Socrates died in **399 BCE**, at approximately seventy years of age.

Plato concludes the *Phaedo* by describing him as the best, wisest, and most just man among those he had known. Yet Plato himself was absent from the final gathering, reportedly because of illness. The famous death scene is therefore a literary account constructed from other testimony and Plato's philosophical understanding of his teacher.

Socrates left **no writings, no formal philosophical institution, and no completed system of doctrines**. What survived was the example of his life, the questions he had raised, and the conversations reconstructed by those who knew him. His influence extended through several philosophical traditions, most importantly through **Plato**, whose Academy helped establish philosophy as a sustained discipline of inquiry.

The historical Socrates remains difficult to separate from the literary figures created by his admirers and critics. Nevertheless, the surviving accounts consistently present an unusually challenging personality: a physically resilient soldier, an unconventional citizen, an intellectually relentless questioner, a companion of politically dangerous men, and a philosopher who made the examination of human life his central occupation.

**Historical sources:** Socrates left no writings of his own. The earliest surviving literary portrayal is Aristophanes' *Clouds* (423 BCE), a comedy that reflects his public reputation but relies heavily on caricature. The most influential accounts are Plato's *Apology* (especially 20e–23b, 28a–32e, 36a–42a), *Symposium* (215a–222b), *Crito* (43a–54e), and *Phaedo* (57a–118a), alongside *Euthyphro*, *Theaetetus*, and other dialogues. Xenophon's *Memorabilia*, *Apology*, and *Symposium* provide an alternative portrait, while his *Hellenica* (1.7) preserves evidence concerning the trial of the Arginusae generals. Important later testimony appears in Aristotle's *Metaphysics* (987b, 1078b) and Diogenes Laertius' *Lives of Eminent Philosophers* (Book II, 18–47). These sources differ substantially in philosophical emphasis, historical purpose, and literary presentation, creating the enduring **Socratic problem**: the difficulty of reconstructing the historical Socrates independently of the interpretations produced by those who wrote about him.
#### Philosophy of Socrates

**The Socratic Problem**
* **Central problem:** Socrates wrote nothing. Every claim about his philosophy depends on authors with their own literary, apologetic, or philosophical aims. The Socratic Problem asks whether, and how far, a coherent philosophical position belonging to the historical Socrates (c. 469–399 BCE) can be recovered from these sources when they disagree. The question matters philosophically and not only historically. Much of what is called "Socratic" philosophy (moral intellectualism, the denial of akrasia, the priority of definition, the elenchus) is identified by separating an "early" Socrates from Plato's later spokesman, and that separation is itself an interpretive hypothesis.
* **Aristophanes' Socrates:** The *Clouds* (produced 423 BCE; the surviving text is a partial revision from a few years later) presents Socrates as head of a "Thinkery" (*phrontistērion*). He studies celestial and subterranean phenomena, replaces Zeus with natural forces such as the Vortex (*Dinos*), and teaches how to make the weaker argument defeat the stronger. The portrait merges three types that were distinct in reality: the natural philosopher, the sophist who teaches rhetoric for pay, and the ascetic eccentric. It is comic caricature, but it matters evidentially. It shows how Socrates could be perceived by contemporaries, and Plato's *Apology* (18a–19d) presents this "old accusation" as the deep source of the formal charges. Its value is as evidence of reputation, not doctrine. The asceticism, barefoot walking, and argumentative habits are corroborated elsewhere; the cosmology and the paid teaching are explicitly denied by both Plato and Xenophon.
* **Xenophon's Socrates:** Xenophon's *Memorabilia*, *Apology*, *Symposium*, and *Oeconomicus* depict a practically minded moral adviser. This Socrates is conventionally pious and emphasizes self-mastery (*enkrateia*), which Xenophon calls the "foundation" (*krēpis*) of virtue at *Memorabilia* I.5.4. He gives positive advice on household management, friendship, and military command. Xenophon's aim is largely apologetic: to show that the charges of impiety and corruption were absurd. This aim plausibly accounts for the blandness of the portrait, but it does not by itself make the portrait unreliable. Xenophon was away from Athens from 401 BCE, and parts of his work may depend on Plato or on the wider genre of *Sōkratikoi logoi*. His Socrates does sometimes pursue definitions, for example at *Memorabilia* IV.6, and does sometimes link virtue with wisdom (III.9.5). He shows little of the systematic disavowal of knowledge or the destabilizing elenchus that dominate Plato's portrait.
* **Plato's Socrates:** Plato's Socrates varies across the corpus. In the dialogues usually called "early" or "Socratic" (*Apology*, *Crito*, *Euthyphro*, *Laches*, *Charmides*, *Protagoras*, *Gorgias*, *Ion*, *Hippias Minor*, and others), he cross-examines interlocutors on ethical definitions, disavows wisdom, and ends many inquiries in aporia. In the "middle" dialogues (*Phaedo*, *Symposium*, *Republic*), he advances the theory of separate Forms, the immortality and tripartition of the soul, and an elaborate political and educational program. In late dialogues he recedes or disappears; he is absent from the *Laws*. The *Meno* and *Euthydemus* are often treated as transitional. The *Meno* in particular introduces recollection and hypothetical method, which go beyond pure elenchus.
* **Aristotle's testimony:** Aristotle was born fifteen years after Socrates' death. He nonetheless supplies the most influential external criterion. *Metaphysics* M.4 (1078b27–30) credits Socrates with two things: inductive arguments (*epaktikoi logoi*) and the search for universal definition. It adds that Socrates did not "separate" universals, whereas Plato did. The *Nicomachean Ethics* reports that Socrates denied akrasia (VII.2) and held the virtues to be forms of practical wisdom or reason (VI.13). Aristotle's evidence is not independent of Plato's dialogues, but it shows that the Academy itself distinguished Socratic from Platonic doctrine on precisely these points.
* **Major interpretive positions:**
  * *Developmentalism (Vlastos):* Gregory Vlastos distinguished "Socrates E" (elenctic) from "Socrates M" (middle period) by a set of contrasting theses. Socrates E is exclusively a moral philosopher; he disavows knowledge, uses only the elenchus, has no theory of separate Forms or tripartite soul, and holds a broadly democratic attachment to Athens. Socrates M has metaphysical and epistemological doctrines, claims knowledge, and holds an anti-democratic political theory. Vlastos argued that the early dialogues give a philosophically coherent and historically credible picture of the real Socrates. He anchored the picture in Aristotle's testimony and in the stylometric grouping of the dialogues.
  * *Proleptic unitarianism (Kahn):* Charles Kahn argues that the "Socratic dialogues" belong to a fictional genre practiced by many Socratics. He holds that Plato composed his apparently early dialogues with the *Republic* already in view, preparing readers for doctrines withheld for pedagogical reasons. On this view the "Socratic" dialogues are no more historical than the others, and the early/middle division reflects a literary strategy rather than intellectual biography.
  * *Skepticism (Dorion and earlier Gigon):* Because all the sources are literary, partisan, and mutually dependent, the historical Socrates' philosophy cannot be reconstructed with any confidence. Scholarship should therefore study the distinct "Socrateses" of Plato, Xenophon, and others as philosophical characters.
* **Assessment:** The relative chronology of the dialogues is far less secure for the "early" group than for the late group established by stylometry. Developmentalism also risks circularity: dialogues are classified as early because they contain "Socratic" doctrines, and those doctrines count as Socratic because they appear in early dialogues. Aristotle's testimony breaks the circle only partially. Still, a moderate core can be stated with some confidence because it is attested across independent or semi-independent sources:
  * Socrates conducted public, ethically focused cross-examination.
  * He professed a distinctive form of ignorance.
  * He claimed a private divine sign.
  * He associated with figures such as Alcibiades and Critias, which damaged him politically.
  * He was tried in 399 BCE on charges of not recognizing the city's gods, introducing new divinities (*daimonia*), and corrupting the young. The wording of the indictment is preserved in Diogenes Laertius II.40, via Favorinus.
  * He held intellectualist views about virtue and wrongdoing.
  
  Beyond this core, one should be explicit that "Socrates" means the character of Plato's Socratic dialogues. This note follows that convention unless it states otherwise.
* **Philosophical significance:** The Socratic Problem is a paradigm case of a general methodological issue in the history of philosophy: the interdependence of interpretation, chronology, and doctrinal attribution. Developmentalist readings shape how one understands Plato's own originality (recollection, Forms, tripartition) as responses to problems left unsolved by the earlier figure. The problem also shows that the "Socratic" philosophy that later schools inherited was already contested in antiquity. This fact helps explain the plurality of the later Socratic schools.

**Socratic Method and Elenchus**
* **Central thesis:** The elenchus (*elenchos*: testing, cross-examination, refutation) is Socrates' characteristic mode of inquiry. The interlocutor proposes an answer to a "What is F?" question or asserts an ethical thesis. Socrates obtains agreement to further premises and derives a contradiction from the total set. The method examines both propositions and persons: what is tested is whether a given life is coherent with the beliefs that guide it.
* **The "What is F?" question and the priority of definition:** In the definitional dialogues Socrates seeks the single form (*eidos*, *idea*) shared by all F things. This form is "that by which" all F things are F (*Euthyphro* 5d, 6d–e). He rejects lists of examples (Euthyphro's prosecution of his father, Laches' "standing firm in battle") because they are neither general nor explanatory. A good definition must state the essence (*ousia*), not a necessary attribute or affection (*pathos*). He also maintains a priority principle: without knowing what F is, one cannot know what F is like, or perhaps whether a particular is F (*Meno* 71b).
* **The Socratic fallacy objection:** P. T. Geach charged Socrates with a "Socratic fallacy." This combines two claims: (A) one cannot know that a predicate applies correctly to anything without knowing a definition of it; (B) a definition cannot be reached by reflecting on examples. Together these make definitional inquiry impossible.
  * *Responses:* Socrates does in fact reason from examples and agreed cases. The priority principle may be restricted to *knowledge* rather than true belief, so that confident but defeasible judgments about cases remain available as starting points. And the principle may concern expert or explanatory knowledge rather than every cognitive grasp of instances. These responses largely dissolve the charge of incoherence. They leave open whether Socrates' own practice fully respects his stated principle.
* **Structure of the standard elenchus:** Vlastos reconstructed the "standard elenchus" as follows.
  1. The interlocutor asserts *p*, a thesis Socrates considers false.
  2. Socrates secures agreement to *q* and *r*, which are not argued for, merely accepted.
  3. Socrates shows that *q* and *r* entail not-*p*.
  4. Socrates concludes that *p* has been shown false.
* **The problem of the elenchus:** Logically, the elenchus establishes only that {*p*, *q*, *r*} is inconsistent. Why reject *p* rather than *q* or *r*? Yet in the *Gorgias* (508e–509a) Socrates claims to have demonstrated, "bound with arguments of iron and adamant," that doing injustice is worse than suffering it.
  * *Vlastos' solution:* Socrates implicitly relies on two assumptions. First, anyone with a false moral belief also holds true beliefs that entail its negation. Second, Socrates' own moral beliefs are consistent, since they have survived repeated testing. Given these, survival through elenchus provides inductive grounds for truth.
  * *Constructivist versus non-constructivist debate:* Critics (Kraut, Benson, Brickhouse and Smith) object that Vlastos' first assumption is not argued for anywhere in the dialogues. They also note that Socrates often speaks of his conclusions as provisional. Hugh Benson argues that the elenchus can establish only doxastic inconsistency. Its function is to test claims to knowledge, not to generate positive doctrine. Brickhouse and Smith allow constructive results but insist they fall short of knowledge.
  * *Significance of the disagreement:* What is at stake is whether the elenchus is a method of discovery or a method of examination, and therefore whether Socrates is a positive moral theorist.
* **The sincerity condition:** Socrates repeatedly insists that interlocutors "say what they believe" (*Crito* 49c–d, *Gorgias* 495a, *Protagoras* 331c). This makes the elenchus person-relative. A refutation tests the interlocutor's own doxastic system and the life organized by it (*Laches* 187e–188a). The condition is also why the method breaks down with Callicles, who answers insincerely to finish the argument, and with Thrasymachus. It explains why elenchus is non-fallaciously *ad hominem*: its conclusion concerns what *this* person cannot coherently hold.
* **Key test cases:**
  * *Euthyphro:* The central argument concerns the dilemma at 10a: is the pious loved by the gods because it is pious, or pious because it is loved? A plausible reconstruction runs:
    1. The pious is loved by the gods because it is pious (agreed).
    2. What is god-loved is god-loved because the gods love it (agreed).
    3. If "pious" and "god-loved" were definitionally identical, they could be substituted in explanatory contexts.
    4. Substitution in (1) yields "the god-loved is loved because it is god-loved," which contradicts (2).
    5. Therefore being god-loved is at most a *pathos* of piety, not its *ousia* (11a–b).
    
    The argument is valid only if definitions must preserve explanatory order, so that explanatory "because" contexts do not license co-extensive substitution. That premise is the most philosophically interesting part of the argument. It underlies later debates about divine command theory and about intensional contexts. Euthyphro's final attempt defines piety as the part of justice concerned with service (*hupēretikē*) to the gods (13d). This founders on the question of what "all-beautiful work" (*ergon*) the gods accomplish through our service, a question the dialogue leaves open.
  * *Laches:* Courage is first defined as "standing firm," which is too narrow: some cavalry tactics involve strategic retreat. Next it is "endurance of soul," which is too broad, since foolish endurance is not admirable. Then it is "wise endurance," which generates counterexamples where wisdom seems to lessen courage. Nicias proposes that courage is knowledge of what is fearful and what is hopeful. Socrates argues that the fearful is future evil, and that genuine knowledge of future goods and evils is inseparable from knowledge of all goods and evils, past and present (198b–199e). Courage would then be the whole of virtue rather than a part of it, contradicting an earlier admission. The refutation thus points directly toward the unity of virtue.
  * *Charmides:* Temperance (*sōphrosynē*) is successively defined as quietness, modesty, "doing one's own," and self-knowledge. The last is refined into "knowledge of itself and of other knowledges" (*epistēmē epistēmēs*). Socrates questions both the possibility and the usefulness of such second-order knowledge. Is any capacity reflexive? Even if knowledge of knowledge were possible, it would be useless without first-order knowledge of good and bad (174b–175a). The dialogue thereby raises the question whether the Socratic "human wisdom" of knowing what one does not know is coherent and valuable.
  * *Meno:* Meno's paradox (80d–e) takes the form of an eristic dilemma. If one knows what one seeks, inquiry is unnecessary; if one does not, one cannot recognize it when found. The geometric demonstration with the slave (82b–85b) shows elenchus reducing false confidence to aporia. Socrates likens this to the numbing of a torpedo fish, the same image Meno had applied to him at 80a–b, and insists the numbing benefits the boy. The demonstration then proceeds constructively toward true belief. Plato supplements the elenchus with three new elements: recollection (*anamnēsis*); a hypothetical method borrowed from geometers (86e–87b); and a distinction between knowledge and true belief, in which true beliefs are "tied down" by reasoning about the cause (*aitias logismos*, 98a). The *Meno* therefore marks the point where the elenchus, by itself, appears insufficient to account for the possibility of inquiry.
* **Aporia and intellectual humility:** Aporia is the impasse in which an interlocutor realizes that they cannot defend what they took themselves to know. It is not merely a negative outcome. It removes the conceit of knowledge, which the later *Sophist* (230b–d) describes as the greatest obstacle to learning, and it motivates further inquiry. The model is Socrates' "human wisdom" in the *Apology* (21a–23b). After Chaerephon reports that the Delphic oracle declared no one wiser than Socrates, Socrates examines politicians, poets, and craftsmen. He concludes that his superiority lies only in not thinking he knows what he does not know.
* **The paradox of Socratic ignorance:** Socrates disavows knowledge of "anything fine and good," yet asserts at *Apology* 29b that he *knows* that doing injustice and disobeying one's superior is bad and shameful. Interpreters differ on how to reconcile these claims.
  * Vlastos distinguishes certain knowledge (knowledge_C), which Socrates disavows, from fallible, elenctically justified knowledge (knowledge_E), which he claims.
  * Woodruff and others distinguish expert from non-expert knowledge.
  * Brickhouse and Smith argue that Socrates lacks the systematic "wisdom" that would make him an expert in virtue, while possessing particular items of moral knowledge.
  
  The related question of Socratic irony (*eirōneia*) concerns whether the disavowal is sincere. Vlastos' "complex irony" holds that the disavowal is true in one sense and false in another. This keeps Socrates honest while explaining the frustration of his interlocutors.
* **Does refutation establish knowledge or merely reveal ignorance?** Taken strictly, an individual refutation reveals inconsistency and therefore exposes a pretense to knowledge. A Socratic thesis that survives many elenchi across many interlocutors acquires a cumulative, defeasible justification. This falls short of the explanatory, systematic knowledge Socrates says only a god possesses fully. The most defensible conclusion is that the elenchus generates warranted moral conviction without definitional knowledge. That gap is what Plato's later epistemology tries to close.

**Ethics and Moral Intellectualism**: `moral intellectualism`, `eudaimonism`
* **Central thesis:** Virtue (*aretē*) is a form of knowledge, specifically knowledge of good and bad. Since all human action aims at what the agent takes to be good, wrongdoing results from ignorance rather than weakness or vice of will. "No one does wrong willingly" (*Gorgias* 509e; *Protagoras* 345d–e, 358c–d).
* **Key concepts:**
  * *Aretē:* The excellence of a thing, the condition that enables it to perform its function well. Applied to humans, it is the condition of soul that makes a person live and act well.
  * *Sophia / phronēsis:* Wisdom. In the Socratic dialogues the terms are used largely interchangeably for knowledge of what is beneficial.
  * *Agathon:* The good, understood primarily as what is beneficial (*ōphelimon*) to its possessor.
  * *Eudaimonia:* Living well or faring well. It is the ultimate end of all rational desire.
* **The eudaimonist axiom:** Socrates assumes that every person wants to fare well and that this desire is the final explanation of all intentional action (*Euthydemus* 278e). The *Meno* (77b–78b) argues that no one desires what they recognize as bad. Those who seem to desire bad things want them under the description "good," because they mistakenly believe them beneficial. In the *Gorgias* (466a–468e), Socrates tells Polus that orators and tyrants do "what seems best to them" but not "what they want." This distinction between apparent and real objects of desire grounds the whole intellectualist program. Wanting (*boulēsis*) is directed at the real good. Error therefore produces action that frustrates the agent's own deepest desire.
* **The argument that virtue is knowledge:** Two parallel arguments are central.
  * *Euthydemus 278e–282d:* Health, wealth, beauty, and power are not good in themselves. They benefit their possessor only when used wisely, and harm when used foolishly. Wisdom is the only thing good in itself; the others are "neither good nor bad" apart from it. This is sometimes read as anticipating the Stoic doctrine of indifferents.
  * *Meno 87d–89a:* The argument can be reconstructed as follows.
    1. Virtue is good and therefore beneficial.
    2. All goods of the soul and body benefit only when guided by wisdom.
    3. What is beneficial in every case must be wisdom, or include it.
    4. Therefore virtue is wisdom, either wholly or in part.
  
  The argument is valid given its premises. Premise (2) carries the weight. It is plausible for instrumental goods, but it assumes that virtue cannot be a non-cognitive disposition, such as a well-trained character, that benefits independently of knowledge. That is exactly the assumption Aristotle later rejects by distinguishing moral virtue (*ēthikē aretē*) from practical wisdom.
* **The craft analogy:** Socrates repeatedly models virtue on technē: medicine, navigation, carpentry. A craft is a teachable body of knowledge directed at a determinate good, able to give an account of its procedures. The analogy explains why virtue should be teachable and why the expert should rule. It also generates difficulties. A craft is a "capacity for opposites": the doctor best able to cure is also best able to poison. The *Hippias Minor* draws the paradoxical consequence that the voluntary wrongdoer is better than the involuntary one, and *Republic* I (333e–334b) concludes that the just person would be the best thief. Virtue, by contrast, cannot be misused. Interpreters therefore disagree whether Socratic moral knowledge is a technē at all. Irwin takes the analogy seriously and reads virtue as craft-knowledge instrumentally productive of happiness. Others, such as Roochnik, take its failures as Socrates' point: moral knowledge is non-technical, self-reflexive, and inseparable from the agent's life.
* **The denial of akrasia (Protagoras 351b–358d):** "The many" believe that one can know what is best yet be "overcome" by pleasure, fear, or love. Socrates instead holds that knowledge is not "dragged around like a slave" by passions (352b–c). His argument has the following structure.
  1. Socrates provisionally adopts a hedonist premise attributed to the many: the good is pleasure and the bad is pain.
  2. Substituting "good" for "pleasure" makes "knowingly choosing the worse because overcome by pleasure" mean "knowingly choosing the worse because overcome by the good," which is absurd.
  3. The phenomenon the many call akrasia is therefore a misjudgment of magnitudes. Near pleasures appear larger than distant ones, as near objects look larger in vision.
  4. The remedy is an "art of measurement" (*metrētikē technē*) that corrects appearances (356d–357b).
  5. Hence no one willingly pursues what they believe worse when a better alternative is available (358c–d).
* **Scholarly debate on the hedonism:**
  * *Does Socrates endorse hedonism?* Some, including Irwin in earlier work and C. C. W. Taylor with qualifications, think the hedonism is Socrates' own, at least in the *Protagoras*. Others (Vlastos, Zeyl) treat it as an ad hominem premise borrowed from the many. They point out that the *Gorgias* (495a–499b) explicitly refutes Callicles' identification of the good with pleasure. The argument's structure survives either reading, because any single evaluative measure supports the same substitution.
  * *Moral psychology (Penner versus Brickhouse and Smith):* Terry Penner defends a "strict intellectualism" in which all motivation is rational desire for the good, plus beliefs about means. Brickhouse and Smith argue that Socrates recognizes non-rational appetites and passions that can distort belief without overriding knowledge. Their view explains why the *Gorgias* recommends punishment and discipline. Akrasia remains impossible as synchronic action against knowledge, but desire can affect what one comes to believe. The issue determines how far Socratic ethics is separated from Plato's tripartite soul in *Republic* IV, where reason, spirit, and appetite can genuinely conflict.
* **Can someone knowingly act against what is good?**
  * *The Socratic answer:* Not if "knowing" means having knowledge. Apparent cases involve either a momentary false judgment about the overall good or a confusion between knowledge and mere belief.
  * *The objection:* The phenomenology of clear-eyed akrasia seems to show a person acting against a judgment they continue to hold, aware of the conflict.
  * *Aristotle's response:* In *Nicomachean Ethics* VII.3, Aristotle partly concedes Socrates' point. The akratic "knows" only in a qualified way, like someone drunk or asleep, and the knowledge that is "dragged about" is not knowledge in the full sense.
  * *Modern formulations:* Donald Davidson's analysis of weakness of will relocates the problem. Akrasia becomes acting against one's all-things-considered judgment in favor of a judgment based on less than all relevant reasons, thereby preserving the motivational force of judgment.
  
  Socratic intellectualism thus remains a live position. It denies that rational agency can be split between judgment and motivation, and it places the burden on the opponent to explain how an agent can act intentionally against the judgment that governs their choices.
* **Unity of virtue:** In the *Protagoras* (329c–334c, 349a–360e), Socrates asks whether justice, piety, temperance, courage, and wisdom are five names for one thing. Alternatively they could be parts, either like the heterogeneous parts of a face or like the homogeneous parts of gold. The dialogue's final argument (359a–360e) proceeds as follows. Courageous people go confidently toward what is genuinely honorable. Cowards are ignorant of what is genuinely fearful. Courage is therefore wisdom about what is and is not to be feared. Combined with the conclusion of the *Laches*, this suggests that each virtue is the knowledge of good and evil applied to a particular domain. Three readings are prominent.
  * *Biconditionality (Vlastos):* The virtues are distinct but inseparable. Anyone who has one has all, but "courage" and "justice" do not mean or name the same thing.
  * *Identity (Penner):* The virtue-names refer to a single psychological state, the knowledge of good and evil. They differ in sense but not in reference, like "morning star" and "evening star."
  * *Hybrid readings:* Each virtue is the same knowledge in different domains, so the virtues are identical in their essential constituent yet distinguishable in application.
  
  Which reading is correct affects whether Socratic "parts of virtue" talk is merely dialectical. It also bears on whether Socrates can explain apparent cases of courage without wisdom, such as courageous criminals.
* **Major objections:**
  * Intellectualism appears to neglect habituation and emotional training, which Aristotle made central to moral education.
  * The craft analogy seems to make knowledge value-neutral. Socrates must explain what distinguishes the knowledge that *is* virtue from mere competence.
  * The *Euthydemus* (291b–292e) shows the problem internally. The "royal art" that is supposed to make people good and wise, and to be the knowledge that matters, apparently has no content beyond itself: "knowledge of what?" This regress is the single most important open problem left by Socratic ethics.
* **Philosophical significance:** Socratic intellectualism set the agenda for Greek moral psychology. Plato's partitioning of the soul, Aristotle's distinction of character and practical wisdom, and the Stoic view of passions as erroneous judgments are all positioned in relation to it. The denial of akrasia remains a standard reference point in action theory. The thesis that virtue is knowledge of what benefits its possessor also inaugurated eudaimonist virtue ethics, the framework within which ancient ethics is debated and which modern virtue ethics revived.

**The Examined Life and Care of the Soul**: `eudaimonism`
* **Central thesis:** The most important human task is care of the soul (*epimeleia tēs psychēs*), making the soul as good as possible. This task requires continual philosophical self-examination, because a person's soul is ordered by their beliefs about what is good. Hence "the unexamined life is not worth living for a human being" (*Apology* 38a).
* **Key concepts:**
  * *Psychē:* In the Socratic dialogues the soul is primarily the seat of moral agency and character, "that which is improved by justice and destroyed by injustice" (*Crito* 47e–48a). Its metaphysical status is left open in the *Apology*, which is agnostic about survival after death. This is very different from the immortal, separable soul argued for in the *Phaedo*.
  * *Self-knowledge:* The Delphic injunction "know thyself" (*gnōthi sauton*) is reinterpreted as knowledge of one's own epistemic condition (the *Apology*'s "human wisdom") and of the state of one's soul. The *Charmides* tests whether such reflexive knowledge is coherent. The *Alcibiades* I, whose authenticity is disputed, identifies the self with the soul and claims that the soul can know itself as an eye sees itself in another's pupil.
  * *Examination (exetasis):* The examined life involves continually testing one's beliefs about how to live in dialogue with others. In the *Apology*, Socrates presents his examining of Athenians as a lifelong service.
* **Argument for the priority of the soul:** In the *Apology* (29d–30b), Socrates rebukes those who care for wealth, reputation, and honor but not for wisdom, truth, and the best state of the soul. The *Crito* (47d–48a) supplies an argument by analogy.
  1. Life is not worth living with a body ruined by disease.
  2. The soul, which is benefited by justice and harmed by injustice, is more valuable than the body.
  3. Therefore life is not worth living with a soul ruined by injustice.
  4. Hence what matters is not living, but living well, which is the same as living justly and finely (48b).
  
  The argument depends on the premise that injustice harms its agent, which is what the *Gorgias* undertakes to prove.
* **The text of Apology 30b:** Socrates' statement at 30b is debated. Traditionally it was read as "from virtue come wealth and all other goods," which would promise material reward for virtue. Burnet and Vlastos prefer the reading "it is virtue that makes wealth and everything else good for human beings." The second reading coheres with the *Euthydemus* thesis that nonmoral goods are good only when used wisely. It is now widely accepted.
* **Argument that doing injustice is worse than suffering it (Gorgias 474b–479e):** Socrates argues against Polus as follows.
  1. Polus concedes that doing injustice is more shameful (*aischion*) than suffering it, though worse (*kakion*) to suffer.
  2. Socrates secures agreement that things are fine (*kalon*) by being either pleasant or beneficial, or both. Correspondingly, things are shameful by exceeding in pain or in badness (harm).
  3. Doing injustice does not exceed suffering it in pain.
  4. So it exceeds in badness and is therefore worse.
  
  Vlastos argued that the inference equivocates. Whose pleasure and benefit make a thing fine? Polus's concession concerns observers' judgments, while the conclusion concerns the agent's own good. Defenders reply that the account of the fine is meant to be agent-neutral, or that the argument's real target is Polus's inconsistent value system. On this second view, the argument shows that Polus cannot coherently separate shame from badness, as the sincerity condition requires.
* **Callicles and the limits of elenchus:** Callicles rejects conventional justice in favor of a "justice of nature" in which the stronger and more intelligent rule and take more. He identifies the good life with maximal gratification of expanded appetites. Socrates' image of the leaky jar and his distinction between good and bad pleasures (495e–499b) force Callicles to concede that some pleasures are bad. This separates pleasure from good. Callicles' eventual withdrawal from sincere participation shows a structural limit of the elenchus. It can compel only someone who accepts the obligation to be consistent and to say what they believe. Socrates had predicted (482b–c) that Callicles would be "in discord with himself" his whole life. The dialogue thereby makes the claim that self-consistency is a precondition of living well, not only of arguing well.
* **Is virtue sufficient for happiness?** At *Apology* 41c–d Socrates says that "no evil can befall a good man, either in life or in death." This seems to entail that virtue is sufficient for happiness. Three readings dominate.
  * *Sovereignty (Vlastos):* Virtue is necessary and sufficient for happiness. Nonmoral goods add small increments ("mini-goods") but cannot outweigh any amount of virtue.
  * *Instrumentalism (Irwin):* Virtue is valued as the craft knowledge that produces happiness, which is distinct from it.
  * *Necessity without sufficiency (Brickhouse and Smith):* Virtue is necessary for happiness, but extreme misfortune could make even a good person's life not worth living. They cite *Crito* 47e on the ruined body.
  
  The debate turns on how to reconcile the uncompromising claims of the *Apology* and *Gorgias* with Socrates' evident ordinary concern for bodily and external goods.
* **Major objections:**
  * The priority of the soul is asserted more forcefully than it is argued for. The analogy between bodily and psychic health presupposes that injustice harms its agent, which is the conclusion in dispute.
  * The examined life seems to make philosophical activity a necessary condition of a worthwhile life. This raises the charge of intellectual elitism against people unable or unwilling to examine their beliefs. A defender can respond that examination need not be technical and that the claim concerns what makes a life worth living *for a human being*, given rational nature, rather than a ranking of persons.
* **Philosophical significance:** The ideal of the examined life gives philosophy a practical and existential function. It anticipates ancient philosophy as a "way of life" (in Pierre Hadot's influential phrase) and modern discussions of reflective endorsement and authenticity. The claim that injustice harms the unjust person remains the core of every attempt to answer the question "Why be moral?" in terms of the agent's own interest. The question is taken up again in *Republic* II, where Glaucon and Adeimantus demand that justice be shown good in itself.

**Justice, Law, and Moral Obligation**
* **Central thesis:** It is never right to do injustice (*adikein*), not even in return for injustice (*antadikein*), nor to do harm in return for harm (*Crito* 49a–e). One must also do what one has justly agreed to do. These principles, not concern for reputation, friends, or survival, determine whether Socrates should escape from prison.
* **The Apology record:** Socrates presents two episodes of principled resistance. As a member of the presiding council (*prytaneis*) in 406 BCE, after the battle of Arginusae, he opposed the illegal collective trial of the generals (32b–c). Under the Thirty, he refused an order to arrest Leon of Salamis for execution (32c–d). He also announces that if the jury acquitted him on condition that he stop philosophizing, he would refuse: "I will obey the god rather than you" (29c–d).
* **The rejection of retaliation:** Traditional Greek morality largely accepted "helping friends and harming enemies," the view Polemarchus defends in *Republic* I and Meno repeats (*Meno* 71e). Socrates' rejection of retaliation breaks decisively with this ethic. He himself acknowledges that few will ever accept it and that those who disagree cannot deliberate in common (*Crito* 49c–d). The argument rests on equating harming (*kakōs poiein*) with doing injustice. This equation is plausible only if harm means making someone worse in respect of human excellence, the interpretation *Republic* I (335b–e) makes explicit.
* **The Laws' speech (Crito 50a–54d):** Socrates imagines the personified Laws of Athens addressing him. They offer three main arguments.
  1. *The destruction argument:* By escaping, Socrates would be attempting, as far as in him lies, to destroy the laws and the city. A city cannot survive if legal verdicts lack force (50a–b).
  2. *The parent argument:* The laws stand to him as parents and masters. They brought about his birth through the laws of marriage, and his nurture and education through other laws. He is therefore not on an equal footing with them and may not retaliate (50d–51c).
  3. *The agreement argument:* By remaining in Athens for seventy years, Socrates tacitly agreed to abide by its laws. He had the option of emigrating and declined it. He preferred Athens even to Sparta and Crete, whose laws he praised (52e). He must therefore either persuade the city of what is just or obey it (*peithein ē peithesthai*, 51b–c, 52a).
  
  The Laws conclude that if Socrates dies, he will have been wronged "not by us, the laws, but by men" (54c).
* **The apparent conflict between Apology and Crito:** Does the *Crito* require unconditional obedience, contradicting the defiance in the *Apology*? Four main resolutions have been offered.
  * *Kraut's reading:* The "persuade or obey" doctrine permits disobedience if one has tried to persuade the city that its command is unjust, including by making one's case in court. The Laws are therefore not authoritarian.
  * *Weiss's reading:* The Laws' speech is not Socrates' philosophical position. It is rhetoric adapted to Crito, who cannot follow philosophical reasoning. Socrates' own reasoning is contained in the dialogue's earlier principles.
  * *Brickhouse and Smith's reading:* There is a distinction between being commanded to *do* injustice, which Socrates would refuse, and being made to *suffer* injustice, which he accepts. The hypothetical order in the *Apology* would not have been a valid legal verdict, and accepting an unjust sentence of death is suffering, not committing, injustice.
  * *Reading with 54c:* The distinction between the laws and the men who misapplied them suggests that escape would wrong the legal order itself, even though the verdict was unjust.
  
  The most plausible synthesis treats the principle of never doing injustice as primary. Escape would violate just agreements and harm the city, whereas accepting an unjust verdict does not require Socrates to commit any injustice.
* **Why Socrates refuses to escape:** Escape would commit injustice against the city and its laws. It would break his agreements. It would contradict the life he defended at trial, making his professed values look hypocritical. It would discredit his philosophy. And it would gain him only a worse life in exile, where his examining would be no more welcome than in Athens (53c–54a).
* **Civil disobedience:** Socrates is often cited as a precursor of civil disobedience; Martin Luther King Jr. invoked him in the *Letter from Birmingham Jail*. The analogy is partial. Modern civil disobedience as theorized by Rawls is public, nonviolent, conscientious, and politically directed. It appeals to a shared public sense of justice to change law or policy, and accepts legal penalty as a sign of fidelity to law. Socrates shares the refusal to commit injustice and the acceptance of penalty. He does not engage in law-breaking as a political strategy. His disobedience (refusing the order against Leon, refusing to stop philosophizing) is a personal refusal to *act* unjustly, not a campaign for legal reform.
* **Major objections:**
  * The agreement argument faces Hume's objection in "Of the Original Contract." Remaining in one's country is not free consent when emigration is very costly. Hume's image is a man carried aboard a ship while asleep, who cannot be said to consent by not leaping into the sea. Socrates' case is unusually favorable to the argument, given his age, wealth of options, and praise of the laws, but the argument does not generalize.
  * The parent analogy suggests a hierarchy incompatible with the reciprocal agreement argument.
  * The destruction argument overstates the effect of a single escape, unless read as a universalizability test: what if everyone disregarded verdicts they believed unjust?
* **Philosophical significance:** The *Crito* contains in compressed form the main families of arguments for political obligation: consent, gratitude and benefit, fair reciprocity, and the necessity of legal order. It also sets the problem of conscientious refusal against those obligations. The principle of non-retaliation is one of Socrates' most radical ethical innovations. It requires that the wrong done to one not license harm to the wrongdoer. It anticipates later prohibitions on revenge and partly motivates the conception of justice as a good of the soul rather than a reciprocal exchange.

**Philosophy, Education, and Politics**
* **Central thesis:** If virtue is knowledge, then political expertise must be knowledge of the good of citizens, and political authority should belong to whoever has that knowledge. Yet Socrates denies that he possesses such knowledge or teaches it. He claims instead that his examining is the "true political art" (*Gorgias* 521d). The resulting tension structures Socratic political thought.
* **Can virtue be taught?**
  * *Protagoras:* Socrates initially argues that virtue cannot be taught (319a–320b). The Athenians consult experts on technical matters but allow anyone to advise on political ones. Distinguished men like Pericles also fail to make their sons virtuous. Protagoras' "Great Speech," with its myth and argument (320c–328d), claims instead that civic virtue is taught by everyone, the way language is learned. By the dialogue's end their positions have reversed (361a–c). Socrates' thesis that virtue is knowledge implies that it is teachable, while Protagoras, who claims to teach it, now denies that it is knowledge.
  * *Meno:* Socrates reasons by hypothesis: if virtue is knowledge, it is teachable. He then argues that there are no teachers of virtue. The sophists are disqualified, as Anytus insists, and great statesmen such as Themistocles, Aristides, Pericles, and Thucydides son of Melesias failed to make their sons good (89e–94e). He concludes that virtue may instead be true opinion, given to statesmen "by divine dispensation" (*theia moira*) without understanding (99e–100b). Many interpreters take this conclusion ironically. The dialogue's ending also marks the gap between Socratic intellectualism and the politics of Athens. Anytus, angered by Socrates' criticism of Athenian notables, was one of Socrates' eventual prosecutors.
* **Socrates' disavowal of teaching:** Socrates denies being a teacher in the sophistic sense (*Apology* 19d–20c, 33a–b). He takes no fees, and he is not responsible for the conduct of anyone who listened to him; implicitly, he means Alcibiades and Critias. The disavowal resolves the apparent paradox only if moral knowledge cannot be transmitted as information but must be generated by each person's own examination. The later image of Socrates as a midwife who brings forth others' ideas without having any of his own (*Theaetetus* 149a–151d) expresses this view. It is a later Platonic image and not obviously the historical Socrates' self-description.
* **Genuine expertise versus rhetoric (Gorgias 462b–465e):** Socrates distinguishes technē, which aims at the good of its object and can give an account (*logos*) of its procedures and causes, from mere empirical knack (*empeiria*, *tribē*). He calls rhetoric a form of flattery (*kolakeia*): it aims at pleasure without understanding. His proportions run as follows.
  * Cookery is to medicine as rhetoric is to justice.
  * Cosmetics is to gymnastics as sophistry is to legislation.
  
  The genuine political art therefore aims at making citizens better. On this basis Socrates condemns the celebrated Athenian statesmen Pericles, Themistocles, Cimon, and Miltiades (515c–517a). By the city's own testimony, they made the Athenians wilder rather than better, gratifying appetites like a pastry cook rather than prescribing like a doctor.
* **Philosophical criticism of political authority:** Socrates explains that he avoided active politics because a just man who opposed the many would have been killed long before (*Apology* 31c–32a). He therefore practiced politics privately, examining individuals.
  * *Was Socrates anti-democratic?* The question is disputed. Xenophon reports the accusation that Socrates criticized the selection of officials by lot (*Memorabilia* I.2.9). I. F. Stone argued that Socrates was hostile to democracy and that his trial was politically motivated. Vlastos argued instead that the Socrates of the early dialogues was a loyal if critical citizen, with no oligarchic sympathies. Kraut emphasizes that Socrates praises Spartan and Cretan laws and that his conception of political expertise is not democratic, yet he chose to remain in Athens.
  * *A balanced view:* The Socratic standard of expertise is epistemic, not demographic. It challenges any regime—democracy, oligarchy, or tyranny—that grounds authority in persuasion, numbers, or force rather than knowledge of the good. Its critique of Athenian democracy is real but internal: Socrates criticizes from within, as a citizen.
* **Major objections:** The epistemic conception of political authority faces an obvious dilemma. If no one has the relevant knowledge, as Socrates' disavowal implies, the expertise model yields no positive political prescription. If someone does, the model seems to license rule by the wise without the consent of the governed, which is the path taken by the philosopher-kings of the *Republic*. A Socratic response is that the absence of experts justifies a politics of examination and humility rather than rule by the wise. This is arguably the deeper lesson of the *Apology* as opposed to the *Republic*.
* **Philosophical significance:** The distinction between knowledge-guided expertise and persuasion aimed at gratification remains basic to debates about rhetoric, propaganda, populism, and the epistemic justification of democracy. Socrates also crystallizes a permanent problem for moral education: if virtue is knowledge, why is it not transmissible? The answers include Plato's recollection and dialectical curriculum, Aristotle's habituation, and the Stoic training of judgment.

**Death, Religion, and Philosophical Integrity**
* **Central thesis:** Socrates combines rigorous rational examination with sincere religious commitment: obedience to the god's mission, trust in his divine sign, and confidence that the gods are wholly good. He faces death without fear, holding that fearing death is a form of pretended knowledge and that no evil can befall a good person.
* **The daimonion:** Socrates describes a "divine something" (*daimonion*), a voice or sign he had experienced from childhood. In Plato's account it only ever turns him away from something (*Apology* 31c–d; also *Phaedrus* 242b–c). It opposed his entering politics. Its silence on the day of his trial is taken as evidence that what was happening to him was good (40a–c). Xenophon's Socrates describes the sign as also giving positive advice. This divergence between sources is significant for the Socratic Problem.
* **Reconciling religion and reason:**
  * *Vlastos' rationalist reading:* Reason remains sovereign. Divine signs and oracles supply only data that require rational interpretation. Socrates did not accept the Delphic pronouncement at face value; he tested it elenctically to understand it (21b–c).
  * *McPherran and Brickhouse–Smith:* Socrates treats divination as a genuine, if non-discursive, source of warrant. His daimonion yields reliable practical guidance that he did not and need not validate through elenchus.
  * *A middle position:* Socrates subordinated religious tradition to rational constraints of consistency and goodness. He rejected stories of divine strife and immorality (*Euthyphro* 6a–c). Yet he accepted that the gods might communicate in ways reason could not fully explicate, while insisting that the gods could not command anything unjust.
* **Was Socrates impious by Athenian standards?** The key Greek term *nomizein theous* is ambiguous. It can mean "believe in gods" or "acknowledge gods in customary worship." Socrates' defense in the *Apology* addresses mainly the first sense, arguing that Meletus's charge of atheism is self-contradictory. Myles Burnyeat argued that Socrates was in fact guilty in the relevant sense. His conception of the gods as wholly good and wise, who cannot be appeased by sacrifice and who demand moral perfection, was incompatible with civic religion understood as reciprocal exchange. If Burnyeat is right, the trial was a real collision between rational ethics and traditional piety, not merely a political pretext. Political motives are also plausible after the defeat of 404 and the Thirty, but the amnesty of 403 barred direct prosecution for political acts before that date.
* **Socratic piety:** Euthyphro's final definition fails, but a positive reconstruction is suggested. Piety would be serving the gods by helping them in their work. That work, presumably the good of human souls, would make Socrates' philosophical mission itself an act of piety. This is how Socrates describes his examining at *Apology* 30a. The reconstruction is a scholarly inference, not something the text asserts.
* **Death:** Socrates offers three considerations against fearing death.
  1. *Epistemic:* Fearing death is thinking one knows what one does not, since no one knows whether death is not the greatest of goods (29a–b).
  2. *Disjunctive (40c–41c):* Death is either annihilation, like a dreamless sleep and therefore a gain, or migration to another place, where one may converse with the great dead and examine them. Either way it is not an evil.
  3. *Theological:* No evil can befall a good man, because the gods do not neglect his affairs (41d).
  
  The disjunctive argument is criticized for assuming that its alternatives are exhaustive and that the afterlife, if any, is benign; Socrates excludes punitive possibilities. Its premise that annihilation is not bad anticipates the Epicurean symmetry and deprivation arguments about death, but there is no clear evidence of direct influence.
* **Phaedo:** The *Phaedo* depicts Socrates' last day and dramatizes his calm and integrity. Its arguments for immortality, however, are Plato's: the cyclical argument, recollection, affinity with the Forms, and the final argument from the Form of Life. They presuppose the theory of separate Forms and a substantial soul distinct from the body. The dialogue's description of philosophy as "practice for dying" (64a, 67e) radicalizes the *Apology*'s agnosticism into an ascetic separation of soul from body.
* **Socrates' last words:** Socrates' last words, "we owe a cock to Asclepius" (118a), have prompted rival interpretations. Nietzsche read them as a confession that life is a disease from which death is the cure. Others take them as a sincere act of gratitude to the healing god, perhaps for the cure of a friend or for deliverance from false opinion.
* **Moral courage and integrity:** Socrates' conduct at trial and in prison exemplifies the "harmony of words and deeds" demanded in the *Laches* (188c–e). He refused to appeal for pity. He proposed, as an alternative penalty, free meals in the Prytaneum, an honor for public benefactors (36d–37a); only then did he offer a fine of thirty minae (38b). According to the *Apology*, the conviction had been narrow: a shift of thirty votes would have acquitted him (36a). His refusal to escape displays integrity, the coherence of a person's actions with the principles they have examined and affirmed.
* **Major objections:** Socrates' certainty about the gods' goodness and his reliance on the daimonion sit uneasily with his professed ignorance. The tension is real and is not resolved in the texts. It can be partly eased by noting that Socrates' religious beliefs are themselves constrained by elenctically secure moral principles: the gods, if wise, must be good.
* **Philosophical significance:** Socrates' death became the paradigm of philosophical integrity in the Western tradition. In Hellenistic consolation literature and especially in Stoicism (Epictetus, Seneca), he serves as the model of the philosopher who cannot be harmed. His religious position, a rationalized theology that moralizes the divine and limits ritual reciprocity, prefigures Plato's critique of poetic theology and the later development of philosophical theology.

**Philosophical Legacy**
* **Central question:** Why did the Socratic tradition produce such fundamentally different philosophical schools? The answer lies in the structure of what Socrates left behind: a practice rather than a doctrine, an exemplary life open to rival interpretations, and formal theses ("virtue is knowledge of the good"; "virtue is sufficient for happiness") whose content he explicitly left undetermined. Each successor resolved this underdetermination differently.
* **Plato and the Socratic dialogue:** Aristotle mentions "Socratic conversations" (*Sōkratikoi logoi*) as a literary genre (*Poetics* 1447b11), practiced also by Xenophon, Antisthenes, Aeschines of Sphettus, and others. Plato transformed the inheritance in several ways.
  * He answered Meno's paradox and the problem of definitional knowledge with recollection and the theory of Forms.
  * He replaced strict intellectualism with a tripartite soul (*Republic* IV) that allows genuine psychic conflict, which makes moral education by habituation and music necessary.
  * He turned the "true political art" into the rule of philosopher-kings.
  * In later works he gradually displaced Socrates as principal speaker.
  
  Plato's Socrates is therefore both the vehicle and the object of Plato's philosophy.
