* **Cynicism:** Antisthenes, an older companion of Socrates, held that virtue is sufficient for happiness, requiring nothing beyond "Socratic strength" (*Sōkratikē ischus*), and that virtue is a matter of deeds rather than argument. Diogenes of Sinope radicalized this into the Cynic ideal of self-sufficiency (*autarkeia*), life according to nature, and shameless defiance of convention. Whether Diogenes was really Antisthenes' student is historically doubtful and may be a Hellenistic construction. Cynicism takes up the Socrates who endured cold and hunger, owned little, and challenged convention, while largely abandoning the dialectical investigation of definitions.
* **Cyrenaicism:** Aristippus of Cyrene, also a companion of Socrates, is traditionally regarded as founder of the Cyrenaic school. It is unclear how much of the doctrine goes back to him rather than to his grandson Aristippus the Younger. The Cyrenaics identified the end (*telos*) with particular present bodily pleasure, understood as a smooth motion. Their epistemology held that only one's own affections (*pathē*) are knowable. Their link to Socrates lies in the ideal of self-mastery over pleasures rather than their abstinence: Aristippus' reported dictum about the courtesan Lais is "I possess, I am not possessed." It also lies in the hedonist calculus of the *Protagoras*, if that is taken as Socrates' own view. Xenophon's *Memorabilia* (II.1) dramatizes a disagreement between Socrates and Aristippus on self-control, which suggests that the divergence was perceived as early as the first generation.
* **Megarian dialectic:** Euclides of Megara combined Socratic ethics with Eleatic monism. Diogenes Laertius reports that he held the good to be one thing called by many names: wisdom, god, intelligence. This suggests a metaphysical version of the unity of virtue. His successors (Eubulides, Diodorus Cronus, Stilpo) developed dialectic into a technical discipline. Eubulides formulated the Liar, Sorites, and Veiled Man paradoxes. Diodorus constructed the Master Argument on modality. Some scholars, following David Sedley, distinguish a "Dialectical school" from the Megarians proper. This branch inherits the argumentative, refutative side of Socratic practice and its interest in the conditions of consistency, detached from Socrates' ethical mission.
* **Stoicism:** Zeno of Citium studied with the Cynic Crates, the Megarian Stilpo, and Diodorus Cronus. Stoicism can be seen as a synthesis of Socratic lines: Cynic ethical sufficiency, Megarian and Dialectical logic, and Socratic intellectualism.
  * Virtue is the only good, and other things are "indifferent" (*adiaphora*), a systematization of the *Euthydemus* argument.
  * Virtue is a form of knowledge, and the virtues are inseparable (Chrysippus' version of the unity thesis).
  * Passions are false evaluative judgments (Chrysippus), so no wise person acts against knowledge, a refined denial of akrasia.
  * The sage is the ideal of complete practical wisdom.
  
  Stoics were content to be called "Socratics," and Epictetus held Socrates up as the model philosopher. A. A. Long has shown how the Hellenistic schools competed over the interpretation of Socrates. Academic skeptics such as Arcesilaus, for example, appealed to Socratic ignorance and to his practice of arguing against every position as precedent for their suspension of judgment.
* **Why the schools diverged:** Four structural features of the Socratic legacy explain the divergence.
  1. *Formal theses without content:* "Virtue is knowledge of the good" leaves open what the good is. The *Euthydemus* regress shows Socrates himself unable to specify it. The candidates his followers chose (pleasure for the Cyrenaics, the One for Euclides, natural self-sufficiency for the Cynics, rational nature for the Stoics, the Form of the Good for Plato) generate different schools.
  2. *Ambiguous exemplarity:* Socrates' life showed both great endurance and evident enjoyment of symposia, friendship, and beauty. It could support both ascetic and hedonist interpretations.
  3. *The tension between ignorance and conviction:* Emphasizing ignorance and elenchus leads toward Academic skepticism. Emphasizing conviction and moral knowledge leads toward Stoic dogmatism.
  4. *Method versus life:* Some heirs inherited primarily the argumentative technique (the Megarians and Dialecticians), others primarily the life (the Cynics), and others tried to integrate both (Plato, the Stoics).
* **Philosophical significance:** The diversity of the Socratic schools is not a sign of distortion of a once-unified doctrine. It shows that Socratic philosophy was a set of problems, commitments, and practices rather than a system. Its core questions remain open in contemporary philosophy:
  * The relation of moral knowledge to motivation.
  * Whether virtue is sufficient for well-being.
  * Whether moral expertise exists and could ground political authority.
  * How self-examination relates to authority, tradition, and religious belief.
  
  Socrates' historical influence is beyond doubt. His philosophical importance lies elsewhere: in the persistence and productivity of these questions, independently of whether the answers attributed to him are correct.

#### Reference

Aristophanes. *Clouds*. Edited by K. J. Dover, Clarendon Press, 1968.
Annotation: Provides the standard edition and commentary on the earliest surviving portrait of Socrates, essential for assessing the comic evidence and the "old accusation."

Plato. *Complete Works*. Edited by John M. Cooper, Hackett Publishing, 1997.
Annotation: Supplies the standard English translations of the *Apology*, *Crito*, *Euthyphro*, *Laches*, *Charmides*, *Protagoras*, *Gorgias*, *Meno*, *Euthydemus*, and *Phaedo* on which the reconstructions are based.

Xenophon. *Memorabilia*. Translated by Amy L. Bonnette, Cornell UP, 1994.
Annotation: Gives the principal non-Platonic portrait of Socrates, indispensable for the Socratic Problem and for the evidence on enkrateia, piety, and the daimonion.

Diogenes Laertius. *Lives of Eminent Philosophers*. Translated by R. D. Hicks, Harvard UP, 1925. 2 vols.
Annotation: Preserves the text of the indictment and much of the biographical and doxographical evidence for Antisthenes, Aristippus, Euclides, and the other Socratic schools.

Vlastos, Gregory. "The Socratic Elenchus." *Oxford Studies in Ancient Philosophy*, vol. 1, 1983, pp. 27–58.
Annotation: Formulates the "problem of the elenchus" and the standard reconstruction that structures the debate about whether refutation can yield moral truth.

Vlastos, Gregory. *Socrates: Ironist and Moral Philosopher*. Cambridge UP, 1991.
Annotation: Develops the developmentalist distinction between the elenctic and the middle-period Socrates, together with influential accounts of irony, the virtue–happiness relation, and Socratic piety.

Kahn, Charles H. *Plato and the Socratic Dialogue: The Philosophical Use of a Literary Form*. Cambridge UP, 1996.
Annotation: Presents the leading proleptic, anti-developmentalist alternative to Vlastos and treats the Socratic dialogue as a literary genre rather than historical record.

Dorion, Louis-André. "The Rise and Fall of the Socratic Problem." *The Cambridge Companion to Socrates*, edited by Donald R. Morrison, Cambridge UP, 2011, pp. 1–23.
Annotation: Surveys the history of the Socratic Problem and argues for the skeptical view that the historical Socrates' philosophy cannot be reconstructed.

Benson, Hugh H. *Socratic Wisdom: The Model of Knowledge in Plato's Early Dialogues*. Oxford UP, 2000.
Annotation: Defends a non-constructive reading of the elenchus and analyzes the priority of definition and the Socratic disavowal of knowledge.

Brickhouse, Thomas C., and Nicholas D. Smith. *Plato's Socrates*. Oxford UP, 1994.
Annotation: Offers a systematic interpretation of Socratic method, ethics, religion, and politics, including the account of the Apology–Crito tension and of virtue as necessary but not sufficient for happiness.

Brickhouse, Thomas C., and Nicholas D. Smith. *Socratic Moral Psychology*. Cambridge UP, 2010.
Annotation: Argues that Socrates recognizes non-rational desires that distort belief, challenging strict intellectualism and illuminating his views on punishment and moral education.

Penner, Terry. "The Unity of Virtue." *The Philosophical Review*, vol. 82, no. 1, 1973, pp. 35–68.
Annotation: Advances the influential identity interpretation of the unity of virtue as a single psychological state of knowledge.

Irwin, Terence. *Plato's Ethics*. Oxford UP, 1995.
Annotation: Reconstructs Socratic ethics within a developmental account of Plato's moral theory, with particular attention to the craft analogy, eudaimonism, and the transition to the tripartite soul.

Kraut, Richard. *Socrates and the State*. Princeton UP, 1984.
Annotation: Gives the classic analysis of the *Crito*'s "persuade or obey" doctrine and of Socrates' relation to Athenian democracy.

Weiss, Roslyn. *Socrates Dissatisfied: An Analysis of Plato's* Crito. Oxford UP, 1998.
Annotation: Argues that the Laws' speech is not Socrates' own philosophical position, providing the main alternative reading of the dialogue's structure.

McPherran, Mark L. *The Religion of Socrates*. Pennsylvania State UP, 1996.
Annotation: Examines Socratic piety, the daimonion, and the reconciliation of religious commitment with rational inquiry.

Burnyeat, M. F. "The Impiety of Socrates." *Ancient Philosophy*, vol. 17, no. 1, 1997, pp. 1–12.
Annotation: Argues that Socrates' moralized conception of the gods made him genuinely guilty of impiety by Athenian civic standards.

Geach, P. T. "Plato's *Euthyphro*: An Analysis and Commentary." *The Monist*, vol. 50, no. 3, 1966, pp. 369–82.
Annotation: Introduces the "Socratic fallacy" objection to the priority of definition, which shaped subsequent discussion of Socratic epistemology.

Long, A. A. "Socrates in Hellenistic Philosophy." *The Classical Quarterly*, vol. 38, no. 1, 1988, pp. 150–71.
Annotation: Traces how Cynics, Stoics, Academics, and Epicureans constructed rival images of Socrates, explaining the divergence of the Socratic tradition.
### Plato ★ (c. 428/427–348/347 BCE)

**Position:** Classical Greek Philosophy · Socratic Tradition · Founder of the Academy · Platonism

* **Name:** Plato (Ancient Greek: Πλάτων, *Plátōn*; /ˈpleɪtoʊ/).
* **Life:** Athenian philosopher, born in Athens or possibly Aegina; died in Athens. A student of Socrates and teacher of Aristotle.
* **Contributions:** One of the foundational figures of Western philosophy. Developed interconnected inquiries into metaphysics, epistemology, ethics, politics, psychology, education, and philosophical method. Founded the Academy in Athens around 387 BCE.
* **Major Works:** *Apology*, *Euthyphro*, *Meno*, *Gorgias*, *Phaedo*, *Symposium*, *Republic*, *Phaedrus*, *Theaetetus*, *Parmenides*, *Sophist*, *Statesman*, *Timaeus*, *Philebus*, *Laws*.

#### Stories about Plato

During the late fifth century BCE, **Athens** was approaching the end of its greatest period of political and cultural influence. The city had become the centre of Greek philosophy, drama, art, and democratic politics, but its long conflict with Sparta was gradually undermining its power. **Plato** was born around 428–427 BCE into a wealthy aristocratic Athenian family, probably in Athens itself, although an ancient tradition places his birth on the island of Aegina. He would witness the defeat of Athens, the collapse and restoration of its democracy, and the execution of **Socrates**, the teacher whose life and death would profoundly shape his philosophical career.

Plato's father, **Ariston**, belonged to a distinguished Athenian family, while his mother, **Perictione**, came from a family traditionally associated with the lawgiver **Solon**. His relatives included **Critias** and **Charmides**, both of whom would become involved in the oligarchic regime established after Athens' defeat in the Peloponnesian War. Plato had two brothers, **Glaucon and Adeimantus**, who later appeared as important characters in his *Republic*, and a sister, **Potone**, whose son Speusippus would eventually succeed him as head of the Academy.

Growing up in an aristocratic household, Plato probably received an education in poetry, music, gymnastics, mathematics, and literature. Ancient biographers describe him as an accomplished athlete who practised wrestling and composed poetry. According to a later tradition, his original name was **Aristocles**, while *Plato* (Πλάτων), derived from the Greek word for "broad," was a nickname referring either to his powerful physique, broad forehead, or expansive literary style. The explanation remains uncertain.

A famous story recounts that the young Plato intended to become a tragic poet. Around the age of twenty, he supposedly encountered **Socrates** near the Theatre of Dionysus. After listening to the philosopher's conversation, he abandoned his literary ambitions and burned the tragedies he had written. The anecdote, preserved by Diogenes Laertius, is probably legendary, but it captures an important feature of Plato's intellectual development: he would eventually combine the dramatic artistry of a poet with the argumentative methods of a philosopher.

Plato became associated with Socrates around the final decade of the fifth century BCE. Unlike the professional Sophists, who charged fees for teaching rhetoric and civic skills, Socrates spent his days questioning the beliefs of those who claimed to possess wisdom. He challenged politicians, poets, craftsmen, and ambitious young men, demanding explanations of justice, courage, virtue, and knowledge.

For the young aristocrat, Socrates presented an unusual example of human excellence. He possessed neither wealth nor political authority, yet he displayed extraordinary intellectual independence, self-control, and moral courage. Rather than seeking public honours, he subjected the assumptions underlying ordinary political life to relentless examination.

Plato's philosophical interests were not limited to Socrates. Aristotle later associated his early intellectual development with **Cratylus**, a follower of Heraclitus, whose philosophy emphasised the instability of the perceptible world. Plato also became deeply interested in the arguments of **Parmenides**, the mathematics of the Pythagoreans, and the theories of earlier natural philosophers. These diverse influences would eventually contribute to his distinctive account of the relationship between changing appearances and stable objects of knowledge.

As a young man from a politically influential family, Plato might reasonably have expected to enter public life. The political crises of his youth, however, complicated that ambition.

In **404 BCE**, Athens surrendered to Sparta, ending the Peloponnesian War. A group of oligarchs, remembered as the **Thirty Tyrants**, seized power and began persecuting political opponents. Among their leaders were Plato's relatives Critias and Charmides.

In the autobiographical account preserved in the disputed **Seventh Letter**, Plato initially hoped that the new government would replace the injustices of the previous regime with a more orderly political system. Instead, he became horrified by its violence and corruption. The oligarchs even attempted to involve Socrates in an unlawful arrest, but the philosopher refused.

When the democracy was restored in **403 BCE**, Plato's disappointment did not disappear. Four years later, the democratic government prosecuted Socrates for impiety and corrupting the young.

Plato was approximately twenty-eight when **Socrates was sentenced to death in 399 BCE**. He appears among the companions who offered to guarantee a fine during the trial, according to his own *Apology*. However, he was absent from Socrates' final gathering in prison, reportedly because of illness.

The execution became one of the decisive events in Plato's intellectual life.

A city celebrated for political freedom and public deliberation had condemned a man whom Plato regarded as exceptionally just. The oligarchs had attempted to compel Socrates to commit injustice; the restored democracy had executed him after a lawful trial. The problem was no longer simply which political faction should govern Athens, but whether any political community could be just without a genuine understanding of justice.

The **Seventh Letter**, whether written by Plato himself or by someone closely acquainted with his political experiences, presents the following conclusion: existing political systems were deeply defective, and meaningful reform required a fundamental change in the relationship between political power and philosophical understanding. Only philosophy could reveal the nature of justice in individual and public life. Political suffering would not end until those who genuinely possessed philosophical wisdom acquired political authority, or rulers themselves became philosophers.

After Socrates' death, Plato reportedly left Athens with several other associates and travelled to **Megara**, where they stayed with **Euclides**, a philosopher associated with the Socratic circle. The historical details of this journey remain uncertain.

Later biographers describe extensive travels to **Cyrene, Egypt, and southern Italy**. Plato supposedly studied mathematics with **Theodorus of Cyrene**, encountered Egyptian religious and scientific traditions, and developed relationships with Pythagorean philosophers. Although these journeys cannot all be verified, his connections with the intellectual communities of southern Italy and Sicily are well established.

Of particular importance was **Archytas of Tarentum**, a Pythagorean mathematician, philosopher, and statesman who combined theoretical knowledge with practical political leadership. Archytas represented a possibility that would remain central to Plato's life: a political leader whose decisions were informed by mathematical and philosophical education.

Around **388–387 BCE**, when Plato was approximately forty, he travelled to **Sicily**, where he visited **Syracuse**, one of the most powerful Greek cities in the Mediterranean. Syracuse was ruled by **Dionysius I**, a formidable tyrant who had built a powerful military state through warfare, diplomacy, and authoritarian rule.

There Plato became closely associated with **Dion**, a young aristocrat and relative of the ruling family. Dion possessed considerable wealth and political influence, but he was also attracted to philosophy. According to the *Seventh Letter*, Plato's conversations deeply affected him, encouraging him to value moderation, self-discipline, and the pursuit of justice above the pleasures and privileges of court life.

Plato's relationship with the older Dionysius was considerably less successful.

Ancient writers describe a confrontation in which Plato criticised the assumption that political power was sufficient to make a ruler good. Dionysius reportedly became furious when the philosopher suggested that tyranny could not be justified merely by the ruler's personal advantage.

A dramatic later tradition claims that Dionysius arranged for Plato to be handed over to **Pollis**, a Spartan envoy, who sold him as a slave on the island of **Aegina**. Plato was supposedly purchased and released by **Anniceris of Cyrene**, who refused to accept reimbursement from his friends. Some versions even claim that money collected for his ransom was used to acquire property associated with the Academy.

The story is memorable because it places one of antiquity's greatest philosophers at a slave market, apparently reduced to merchandise through the anger of a tyrant. Its historical reliability is doubtful, however, and the circumstances of Plato's departure from Sicily remain uncertain.

Plato returned to Athens and, around **387 BCE**, established the institution that would become known as the **Academy**.

The Academy took its name from a gymnasium and sacred grove associated with the hero **Academus**, located outside the city walls of Athens. Plato conducted philosophical activities there and possessed property nearby. The Academy gradually developed into a community devoted to intellectual inquiry, attracting students and collaborators from different parts of the Greek world.

Unlike a modern university, it did not possess a system of formal degrees or a curriculum documented in administrative records. Nevertheless, its members investigated mathematics, astronomy, ethics, political philosophy, logic, and the nature of reality. Mathematical inquiry occupied an especially prominent position, reflecting Plato's conviction that abstract reasoning could train the mind to recognise relationships that ordinary perception could not reveal.

A later tradition claims that an inscription above the Academy's entrance warned those ignorant of geometry not to enter. The inscription is not securely attested from Plato's lifetime, but it became an influential symbol of the Academy's mathematical orientation.

Plato's teaching also differed from the public questioning associated with Socrates. While discussion and argument remained central, the Academy developed increasingly sophisticated methods of conceptual analysis, mathematical reasoning, and philosophical investigation. Its members did not necessarily accept all of Plato's conclusions. Disagreement was an important part of the intellectual environment.

One ancient anecdote, preserved through Aristoxenus, describes Plato delivering a lecture **On the Good**. Members of the audience supposedly expected to hear about conventional goods such as happiness, wealth, and health. Instead, Plato discussed mathematics, numbers, geometry, and the unity underlying reality. Many listeners found the lecture bewildering.

The story illustrates the distance between Plato's conception of philosophical knowledge and ordinary expectations. To understand what makes something good, he believed, required more than collecting examples of desirable things. It required investigating the principles that made such judgements possible.

At the Academy, Plato also developed the literary form that made him one of the most influential writers in Western intellectual history: the **philosophical dialogue**.

Unlike most philosophers, Plato rarely presented arguments directly in his own name. Instead, he created conversations involving recognisable historical figures, including Socrates, Protagoras, Gorgias, Parmenides, Alcibiades, and his own relatives. These dialogues combine dramatic settings, intellectual conflict, humour, irony, and rigorous philosophical reasoning.

In *Apology*, *Crito*, and *Phaedo*, he reconstructed the trial, imprisonment, and death of Socrates, transforming his teacher into one of philosophy's enduring literary figures. In *Symposium*, he situated philosophical discussions of love within an Athenian drinking party, where Socrates eventually explained the ascent from attraction to individual beauty towards contemplation of **Beauty itself**.

His most ambitious political work, the ***Republic***, developed through an imagined conversation between Socrates and several interlocutors, including Plato's brothers Glaucon and Adeimantus. Beginning with the question of whether justice benefits the just person, the discussion expands into an investigation of political institutions, education, human psychology, knowledge, and reality.

In one of its most famous passages, **the Allegory of the Cave**, Plato presents human beings imprisoned underground, able to see only shadows projected on a wall. Having never encountered anything else, they mistake these shadows for reality.

One prisoner is released and gradually discovers the objects responsible for the shadows, the world outside the cave, and eventually the sunlight illuminating everything. When he returns to explain his discovery, the remaining prisoners resist him and may even become hostile.

The allegory illustrates Plato's conviction that education is not simply the transmission of information, but a transformation of the soul's orientation towards reality. It also recalls the fate of Socrates: someone who challenges familiar beliefs may be rejected or persecuted by the very people he hopes to enlighten.

Plato's metaphysical investigations developed into the **Theory of Forms**, according to which changing particular things are understood through stable, intelligible realities such as Justice, Beauty, and Equality. His intellectual project drew together the Socratic search for definitions, the Eleatic demand for rational coherence, and the mathematical traditions of the Pythagoreans.

Yet Plato did not merely defend a fixed doctrine. In *Parmenides*, he presented powerful objections to the theory of Forms through a fictional encounter between the elderly Parmenides and a young Socrates. In *Theaetetus* and *Sophist*, he examined knowledge, falsehood, being, and non-being through increasingly complex arguments. His writings repeatedly subjected philosophical conclusions to further examination.

Around **367 BCE**, when Plato was approximately sixty, a seventeen-year-old student named **Aristotle** arrived in Athens from **Stagira** in northern Greece. Aristotle entered the Academy and remained associated with it for approximately twenty years.

Although Aristotle eventually developed philosophical positions that challenged important Platonic doctrines, his education at the Academy profoundly shaped his intellectual methods. Their relationship would become one of the most consequential teacher-student relationships in Western philosophy.

But Plato's life was not confined to philosophical discussion and literary composition. His earlier friendship with Dion continued to draw him into the dangerous politics of Syracuse.

In **367 BCE**, Dionysius I died, and his son **Dionysius II** inherited the government. The young ruler had received relatively little preparation for political leadership. Dion, who possessed considerable influence at court, believed that Plato might educate him and encourage the establishment of a more just government.

Dion invited Plato to return to Syracuse.

The philosopher was now in his early sixties. He had spent decades developing arguments about justice, education, and political authority. Syracuse offered an extraordinary opportunity to test whether philosophy could influence the government of an actual state.

The *Seventh Letter* presents Plato as initially reluctant. He doubted whether the young tyrant possessed the temperament necessary for serious philosophical study and understood the dangers of court politics. Yet he feared that refusing the invitation would expose his political philosophy to the accusation that it consisted only of words, without the courage to attempt practical reform.

Plato therefore returned to Sicily around **367–366 BCE**.

At first, the prospects appeared encouraging. Dionysius II displayed interest in philosophy and spent time discussing mathematics and political principles with his distinguished guest. Plutarch describes an atmosphere in which geometry briefly became fashionable at court, with mathematical diagrams appearing among the courtiers.

The enthusiasm soon encountered political resistance.

Dion's growing influence aroused suspicion among rival advisers, who accused him of conspiring against the tyrant. Dionysius eventually **exiled Dion**, removing Plato's principal political ally from Syracuse.

Plato found himself in an increasingly uncomfortable position. Dionysius valued his prestige and sought his approval but resisted the personal discipline and political reforms associated with his teaching. The tyrant's attitude towards Plato became a mixture of admiration, possessiveness, suspicion, and resentment.

Plutarch portrays Dionysius almost as a jealous admirer who wished to monopolise the philosopher's attention. He wanted Plato to favour him above Dion, yet feared that serious philosophical education would undermine his political authority.

The situation exposed a practical difficulty in Plato's ideal of philosophical government: a ruler might desire the reputation of wisdom without accepting the intellectual discipline or moral transformation that genuine wisdom required.

Plato eventually obtained permission to leave Syracuse and returned to Athens, where he reunited with the exiled Dion.

Dion spent several years associated with the Academy. Plato reportedly encouraged him to associate with **Speusippus**, his own nephew, hoping that their friendship would soften Dion's severe temperament. Plutarch also records that Dion helped finance a public chorus organised by Plato, illustrating the continued closeness of their relationship.

Around **361 BCE**, Dionysius II again invited Plato to Syracuse. The ruler claimed to have developed a renewed interest in philosophy and appeared willing to reconsider his relationship with Dion.

Plato, now in his late sixties, was reluctant to return. Nevertheless, pressure from Dion and assurances from the Pythagorean circle associated with Archytas persuaded him to undertake a third Sicilian journey.

This final visit proved even more dangerous.

Dionysius failed to restore Dion to his former position and eventually interfered with his property. Plato's attempts at mediation achieved little. The tyrant increasingly restricted his movements, and the philosopher reportedly feared for his personal safety.

According to Plutarch, **Archytas and his associates intervened**, sending a ship and diplomatic representatives to secure Plato's release. Dionysius eventually permitted him to depart.

A later anecdote describes the tyrant asking whether Plato would return to Athens and speak badly of him. Plato supposedly answered that the Academy had too many important subjects to discuss to waste time talking about Dionysius.

Whether or not the exchange occurred, it became a memorable expression of the philosopher's independence from political power.

Plato returned to Athens around **360 BCE**, ending his direct involvement with the Syracusan court.

His political difficulties, however, did not end with his departure.

In **357 BCE**, Dion organised a military expedition against Dionysius II and successfully entered Syracuse. His victory initially appeared to offer the possibility of political reform, but the situation rapidly deteriorated. Rival factions, popular opposition, and continuing military struggles prevented the establishment of a stable government.

Dion's own conduct became increasingly controversial. In **354 BCE**, he was assassinated by conspirators led by **Callippus**, an Athenian associate who had previously maintained friendly relations with him.

The death was a severe blow to the political project with which Plato had been associated. Dion had been his friend, student, and principal hope for philosophical reform in Syracuse. Yet Dion's attempt to transform political authority had ended in violence, instability, and assassination.

The *Seventh Letter* presents a retrospective defence of Plato's involvement in these events, addressed to Dion's surviving associates. Whether authentic or written by a knowledgeable member of the Platonic circle, it records the tension between philosophical ideals and the realities of political action.

Plato's Sicilian experiences therefore complicate any simple image of him as a philosopher concerned only with abstract ideas. He had repeatedly attempted to influence a powerful ruler, travelled across the Mediterranean in pursuit of political reform, and experienced the vulnerability of intellectual independence within an authoritarian court.

During his final years, Plato continued teaching and writing at the Academy. His later dialogues display increasingly intricate philosophical arguments and an expanded interest in classification, mathematics, cosmology, and legislation.

In the ***Timaeus***, he developed a cosmological account in which a divine craftsman, the **Demiurge**, orders the universe according to intelligible mathematical principles. In the *Critias*, he introduced the famous story of **Atlantis**, a powerful island civilisation whose conflict with ancient Athens formed part of a larger philosophical narrative. The story would later acquire an independent legendary existence far beyond its original literary context.

In the ***Laws***, probably his last major work, Plato returned to the practical problems of political organisation. Unlike the *Republic*, which centres on the possibility of government by genuine philosophical wisdom, the *Laws* presents a detailed constitutional and legislative programme for a hypothetical city. Its discussions concern education, property, religion, criminal law, political institutions, and the maintenance of civic order.

The differences between these works should not be reduced to a simple abandonment of idealism. Nevertheless, they reveal Plato's continued investigation of how political life might be organised when the extraordinary wisdom required of philosopher-rulers cannot be assumed.

According to a later tradition, Plato continued revising the *Laws* until his death, leaving the work on wax tablets. His associate **Philip of Opus** was subsequently connected with its preparation and transmission.

Ancient biographers also preserved stories concerning Plato's personality and final years. He was remembered as a disciplined, intellectually demanding man who valued moderation and serious philosophical discussion. His aristocratic background and association with wealthy patrons did not prevent later writers from presenting him as critical of luxury and excessive attachment to material possessions.

His intellectual reputation had grown throughout the Greek world. Students and scholars came to the Academy from different cities, and his writings circulated beyond Athens. Unlike Socrates, whose philosophy survived largely through the testimony of others, Plato created an extensive literary body of work that preserved and developed his thought.

Plato died in **348–347 BCE**, at approximately eighty years of age.

One ancient tradition claims that he died while attending a wedding feast, while other accounts provide different circumstances. None can be accepted with confidence. He was buried in or near the Academy, where he had spent much of his adult life.

His surviving will, preserved by Diogenes Laertius, records property, money, household possessions, and legal arrangements. These relatively ordinary details offer a striking contrast to the later legends portraying Plato as an almost divine intellectual figure.

Following his death, **Speusippus**, his nephew, became head of the Academy. **Aristotle**, who had studied there for approximately twenty years, subsequently left Athens and pursued his own philosophical career. The Academy itself continued to operate for centuries, undergoing substantial changes in its doctrines and intellectual orientation.

Plato left an extraordinary philosophical and literary inheritance. He transformed the conversations of Socrates into a form of writing capable of examining nearly every major question of human existence: what knowledge is, what reality consists of, what makes a life good, whether the soul survives death, how justice should be understood, and who should possess political authority.

His life also contained an unresolved tension between philosophical understanding and political action. He had witnessed the failures of oligarchy and democracy, attempted to educate a tyrant, founded an institution dedicated to intellectual inquiry, and watched his closest political associate die in a struggle for power.

The contrast is central to his historical character. **Plato was both the philosopher who imagined government guided by wisdom and the political adviser who discovered how difficult it was to make rulers wise.**

**Historical sources:** Plato's philosophical writings survive extensively, but he rarely speaks directly in his own voice. His principal ancient biography appears in Diogenes Laertius' *Lives of Eminent Philosophers* (Book III, especially 1–7, 18–25, and 37–43), which preserves family traditions, intellectual relationships, travels, anecdotes, and accounts of his death, although many details are unreliable. The **Seventh Letter** (especially 324b–326b and 327a–352a) provides the most substantial account of his political ambitions and Sicilian experiences, but its authenticity remains disputed; even if it is not Plato's own work, it may preserve historically valuable information from his circle. Plutarch's *Life of Dion* (especially 4–20 and 47–57) provides a detailed, though later and sometimes partisan, narrative of his relationship with Dion and the Syracusan rulers. Aristotle's *Metaphysics* (987a–b) and *Politics* (Book II, Chapter 6) offer particularly important near-contemporary testimony concerning Plato's philosophical development and political ideas. Additional information appears in Cicero, Diodorus Siculus, and later Neoplatonic biographies. The central outlines of Plato's life—his aristocratic background, association with Socrates, establishment of the Academy, involvement in Sicilian politics, and relationship with Aristotle—are well supported, while many personal anecdotes, precise chronologies, and explanations of his motives remain uncertain.

#### Philosophy of Plato

**Philosophical Method and Dialectic**
* **Central thesis:** Philosophical knowledge cannot be acquired by passive reception or rhetorical persuasion. It is reached through disciplined dialogue (*dialegesthai*), whose method Plato progressively reconstructs. It develops from Socratic refutation, to hypothetical reasoning, to a dialectic aimed at an unhypothetical first principle, and finally to the systematic articulation of kinds by collection and division. The successive forms of method track Plato's changing views about what knowledge is and what its objects are.
* **Elenchus and its limits:** Plato's earliest works present the elenchus: cross-examination that exposes inconsistency in an interlocutor's beliefs and produces aporia. Its value is cathartic. It removes the conceit of knowledge, which the *Sophist* (230b–d) later calls a "noble sophistry" that purifies the soul. But it shows only that a set of beliefs is inconsistent, not which belief is false. Meno's paradox (*Meno* 80d–e) generalizes the difficulty: if one neither knows nor can recognize what one seeks, how can inquiry reach positive knowledge? Plato's methodological development can be read as a sequence of responses to this gap.
* **Hypothetical method:** In the *Meno* (86e–87b) Socrates borrows from geometers the procedure of investigating a question "from a hypothesis": *if* virtue is knowledge, *then* it is teachable. The *Phaedo* (99c–101e) presents a "second sailing" (*deuteros plous*). One posits the account (*logos*) judged strongest. One accepts what agrees (*sumphōnein*) with it and rejects what does not. If the hypothesis itself is challenged, one defends it by a higher hypothesis, "until one reaches something adequate" (*hikanon*). The method yields conditional, revisable knowledge whose security depends on how far the chain of hypotheses has been grounded.
* **Dialectic in the Republic:** *Republic* VI–VII describe dialectic as the culmination of education.
  * Mathematics takes its hypotheses as starting points and proceeds downward to conclusions without giving an account of them. Dialectic treats hypotheses as "stepping-stones" and ascends to an "unhypothetical first principle" (*archē anhupothetos*, 511b), the Form of the Good. It then descends again, using only Forms, without sensible images.
  * Dialectic "does away with" (*anairei*) hypotheses (533c), presumably by removing their merely hypothetical status through derivation from the first principle.
  * The dialectician can give an account of the being of each thing (534b) and is "synoptic," able to see the interconnections among the sciences (537c).
  
  The model is foundationalist in shape. Gail Fine and others nonetheless argue that the knowledge it describes is holistic or coherentist: one knows each Form by understanding its place in a whole structured by the Good. Richard Robinson questioned whether an unhypothetical principle is intelligible at all. Socrates himself says he can offer only images of the Good, not dialectical knowledge of it (506e, 533a).
* **Collection and division:** In the *Phaedrus* (265d–266b), dialectic becomes the twin procedure of collection (*sunagōgē*) and division (*diairesis*). Collection gathers scattered instances under a single form. Division cuts that form "at its natural joints" rather than hacking it "like a bad butcher." Three later dialogues develop the method.
  * *Sophist and Statesman:* Repeated bifurcatory divisions produce definitions, starting from the paradigm of the angler. The *Sophist* generates seven divergent definitions of the sophist, which shows that division alone does not guarantee a unique definition.
  * *Statesman* 262c–263a: The Visitor warns against dividing humanity into Greeks and barbarians, because a mere part (*meros*) is not a kind (*eidos*). Division must follow real joints in reality, so its success presupposes ontological knowledge it cannot itself supply.
  * *Philebus* 16c–18d: A "gift of the gods" method requires finding the precise number of kinds between the one and the unlimited. The illustration is Theuth's classification of letters into vowels, semivowels, and mutes.
* **Aristotle's critique of division:** Division cannot demonstrate. At each step it begs the question of which branch the definiendum falls under. Aristotle calls it a "weak syllogism" in the *Prior Analytics* and develops the critique in the *Posterior Analytics*. Division is a means of articulating and organizing knowledge rather than discovering it.
* **Dialectic, rhetoric, and sophistry:**
  * *Gorgias:* Rhetoric as commonly practiced is an empirical knack (*empeiria*), a form of flattery aimed at pleasure without understanding. Dialectic tests beliefs for truth through question and answer.
  * *Phaedrus:* Plato allows a philosophical rhetoric. Its practitioner must know the truth about the subject, define and divide it, know the types of souls, and match types of speech to types of soul (271c–272b, 277b–c).
  * *Sophist:* The sophist is a producer of images (*eidōla*) and appearances, a mimic of the wise who argues in order to win. The difficulty of defining the sophist leads directly into the ontology of falsehood and non-being (see below).
  
  The underlying distinction is between discourse governed by truth and the good of the soul, and discourse governed by victory or gratification.
* **The critique of writing:** In the *Phaedrus* (274c–277a), the Egyptian king Thamus rejects Theuth's invention of writing on two grounds. It produces the appearance of wisdom without understanding, and it weakens memory. A written text "always says the same thing," cannot answer questions, and cannot defend itself. Genuine discourse is "written in the soul" of the learner by dialectic. This passage is central to debates about why Plato wrote dialogues rather than treatises, and about the "unwritten doctrines" (see the final section).
* **Major objections:** The progression from elenchus to dialectic is a scholarly reconstruction, not something Plato states. It depends on assumptions about chronology. Each method also carries an unsolved problem.
  * Elenchus cannot identify which belief is false.
  * Hypothetical method can rest on unexamined premises.
  * The unhypothetical principle is never shown to be attainable.
  * Division presupposes the knowledge of natural kinds it is meant to articulate.
* **Philosophical significance:** Plato's methods established the main models for systematic inquiry in the Western tradition: refutation by inconsistency, inquiry by hypothesis, axiomatic grounding, and taxonomy by genus and differentia. Aristotle's dialectic, Euclid's axiomatics, and the Porphyrian tree of genera and species are recognizable descendants. Plato's insistence that method must be answerable to the structure of reality, rather than merely to argumentative success, defines the difference between philosophy and eristic.

**Metaphysics and Ontology**: `Platonic realism`
* **Central thesis:** Besides the changing, perceptible world, there exist Forms (*eidē*, *ideai*). Forms are eternal, unchanging, intelligible natures, such as Beauty itself, Justice itself, and Equal itself. Each Form is purely and completely what it is. Sensible things are what they are by "participating" in Forms. Forms are the primary objects of knowledge, the causes or explanations (*aitiai*) of the properties of particulars, and the paradigms by which particulars are measured. The supreme principle of this structure is the Form of the Good.
* **Key concepts:**
  * *Ousia* and *to on*: being, or what is.
  * *Auto kath' hauto*: "itself by itself." This phrase marks a Form's independence from particulars.
  * *Monoeidēs*: "uniform." A Form is a single nature, not compounded or varying.
  * *Methexis*: participation.
  * *Paradeigma*: paradigm or model.
  * *Mimēsis* / *homoiōma*: imitation or likeness. Particulars are likenesses of Forms.
  * *Chōrismos*: separation. This is Aristotle's term; Plato's own language is "itself by itself" and the distinction between being and becoming.
* **Characteristic descriptions:** The *Symposium* (211a–b) describes Beauty itself. It always is; it neither comes to be nor passes away. It is not beautiful in one respect and ugly in another, nor beautiful to some and ugly to others. It is "itself by itself, with itself, uniform." The *Phaedo* (78c–80b) describes Forms as unchanging, invisible, and grasped only by reasoning. The *Timaeus* (27d–28a) contrasts "what always is and has no becoming" with "what becomes and never is." The first is grasped by understanding with an account; the second by opinion together with perception.
* **Arguments for Forms:** Plato never gives one master argument. Scholars reconstruct several.
  * *The argument from the compresence of opposites:* This argument is most explicit in *Republic* V (479a–c) and *Phaedo* 74b–c. Any sensible beautiful thing is also, in some respect, relation, or comparison, ugly. Equal sticks appear equal to one thing and unequal to another. What is both F and not-F cannot be what F-ness is, and cannot explain why things are F. Therefore there must be something that is F without qualification: the Form. The argument requires that the predicates concerned be incomplete or relational: large, equal, beautiful, just.
  * *The argument from flux:* Aristotle reports that Plato accepted the Heraclitean view, learned from Cratylus, that sensibles are in constant change. Plato concluded that knowledge requires stable non-sensible objects. The *Cratylus* (439c–440d) argues that if everything were in flux, nothing could be known or even named. Terence Irwin distinguished "aspect change," which is the compresence of opposites, from "self-change," which is alteration over time. He argued that Plato's main concern is the former.
  * *One over many:* *Republic* X (596a) states the practice of positing "a single Form for each set of many things to which we apply the same name." This principle generates the problem of scope. In the *Parmenides* (130b–e), young Socrates is confident there are Forms of likeness, one, many, just, beautiful, and good. He is uncertain about human being, fire, and water, and reluctant to posit Forms of hair, mud, and dirt.
  * *Aristotle's reconstruction:* In the lost treatise *On Ideas*, partly preserved by Alexander of Aphrodisias, Aristotle reconstructs several Platonic arguments. One is from the sciences: every science has a single object that is not a particular. Another is from thinking: we can think of man when no man is present. Aristotle argues that the "more accurate arguments" lead either to Forms of relatives or to the Third Man regress.
* **Being, becoming, and degrees of reality:** Plato says that Forms "are" in a way sensibles are not. Interpreters disagree how to read "is" (*einai*) here.
  * *Existential:* Forms exist more fully than sensibles.
  * *Predicative:* Forms are F completely, whereas particulars are F only qualifiedly.
  * *Veridical:* Forms are what is true or real.
  
  Vlastos's "degrees of reality" analysis and Charles Kahn's studies of the Greek verb *einai* led to the predominant view that the contrast is primarily predicative and explanatory. Forms are fully and unqualifiedly F; particulars are F only in some respects and at some times. This reading lessens the apparent strangeness of "degrees of existence."
* **Participation and the one-many problem:** The *Phaedo* (100b–e) gives the "safe but simple" explanation: beautiful things are beautiful by Beauty. Socrates expressly declines to say whether the relation is presence (*parousia*), communion (*koinōnia*), or something else (100d). Plato's metaphors vary: participation, resemblance to a model, the presence of the Form in the thing. Each faces difficulties that the *Parmenides* makes explicit (see "Critical Examination of the Forms"). A related problem concerns how one thing can be many. How can one particular bear many predicates? How can one Form be in many particulars? How can a Form be one while being related to many other Forms? This problem runs through the *Parmenides*, *Sophist*, and *Philebus* (14c–15c).
* **The Form of the Good:** In *Republic* VI (505a–509b), the Good is the "greatest object of study" (*megiston mathēma*). Without knowing it, knowing everything else is useless. Socrates offers only images of it.
  * *Sun analogy:* The sun makes visible things visible and causes their generation and growth. The Good similarly makes the Forms knowable and is the source of their being (*to einai kai tēn ousian*). The Good itself "is not being, but beyond being in dignity and power" (*epekeina tēs ousias*, 509b).
  * *Interpretations:*
    * *Neoplatonic:* The phrase "beyond being" is read literally. The Good is the One, transcending being and intellect.
    * *Formal or teleological:* The Good is the principle of order, unity, and functional excellence. Each Form is what it is by being a determinate, well-ordered nature, so the Good explains the Forms' being by explaining their structure and intelligibility.
    * *Deflationary:* "Beyond being" is hyperbole for the Good's preeminence among the Forms.
  * *The lecture "On the Good":* Aristoxenus reports that the audience, expecting a discussion of human goods, was baffled by a lecture on mathematics, astronomy, and the thesis that "the Good is One." This connects the *Republic*'s Good with the "unwritten doctrines."
* **Why intelligible reality is more fundamental:** Plato's reasons can be summarized in four claims.
  * *Explanatory priority:* Forms explain why particulars have their properties; particulars do not explain Forms.
  * *Epistemic priority:* Knowledge requires stable, determinate objects. Only Forms satisfy this requirement, and stability of meaning also depends on them.
  * *Ontological dependence:* Particulars are likenesses or images whose being is derivative.
  * *Axiological priority:* The intelligible order is structured by the Good, and the sensible world is good insofar as it reflects that order.
* **Major objections:**
  * *Aristotle's general critique:* Forms are a "duplication" of the world. Positing them does not explain generation, change, or knowledge of sensibles. To say particulars "participate" in Forms is to use "empty words and poetic metaphors" (*Metaphysics* A.9).
  * *The Third Man:* If Forms are self-predicating paradigms, a regress threatens (see "Critical Examination of the Forms").
  * *Separation:* If Forms are entirely separate, the relation between them and particulars becomes unintelligible.
  * *Scope:* There appears to be no principled criterion for which predicates correspond to Forms.
* **Philosophical significance:** The theory of Forms is the origin of the problem of universals and of realism about abstract objects. It established a set of lasting questions: how predication works, how properties relate to their instances, how explanation works, and whether the intelligible is metaphysically prior to the sensible. Medieval realism, early modern rationalism, Frege's "third realm," and contemporary platonism about mathematics and properties all develop or contest Platonic positions. These later views are not continuous with Plato's theory, which includes paradigmatism, value, and causal explanation in ways modern platonisms generally do not.

**Epistemology**: `rationalism`, `innatism`
* **Central thesis:** Knowledge (*epistēmē*) differs in kind from opinion or belief (*doxa*). It is stable, explanatory, and grounded in understanding of why things are so. Its primary objects are Forms, and it is attained by reasoning, not by perception. Plato explains the possibility of inquiry by the doctrine of recollection (*anamnēsis*): learning is the recovery of knowledge the soul possessed before birth.
* **Key concepts:**
  * *Epistēmē:* Knowledge, often closer to "understanding" than to modern propositional "knowing that." It typically involves grasping explanations (*aitiai*) and being able to give an account (*logos*).
  * *Doxa:* Opinion or belief. It can be true (*alēthēs doxa*) or false, and it is unstable unless anchored by explanation.
  * *Logos:* Account, explanation, or definition.
  * *Aisthēsis:* Perception.
* **Recollection:**
  * *In the Meno (81a–86c):* Socrates responds to Meno's paradox by citing priests and poets. The soul is immortal and has learned all things, so seeking and learning are recollecting. The slave-boy demonstration (82b–85b) is offered as evidence. An uneducated boy, by answering questions about doubling the area of a square, first falls into aporia and then arrives at true belief about the diagonal. Since he was not taught the answer, the true beliefs must have been "in him." Repeated questioning would turn them into knowledge.
  * *In the Phaedo (72e–77a):* The argument runs roughly:
    1. Sensible equals (sticks, stones) are deficiently equal; they "fall short" of Equal itself.
    2. To judge that something falls short of a standard, one must already know the standard.
    3. We judge sensible equals deficient from our first use of the senses.
    4. Therefore we knew Equal itself before we began to perceive, which is to say before birth.
    5. Perception "reminds" us of the Form.
    
    The argument depends on the premise that recognizing deficiency requires prior knowledge of the standard. It also depends on the assumption that this recognition occurs from birth, which Plato never examines.
  * *Scope of recollection:* Dominic Scott distinguishes two interpretations. On the "Kantian" or ordinary-concept-formation reading, recollection explains how everyone acquires concepts. On what he calls the "D" reading, the latent knowledge is recollected only through philosophical inquiry; ordinary concept use is learned from experience and convention. The question bears on whether recollection is a general theory of concept acquisition, an ancestor of Leibniz's innatism, or a narrower account of philosophical understanding.
* **Knowledge and true belief:** In the *Meno* (97a–98a), a person with true belief about the road to Larissa guides as well as one with knowledge. True beliefs are valuable but "run away" like the statues of Daedalus unless they are "tied down by reasoning about the cause" (*aitias logismos*). Once tied, they become knowledge. Edmund Gettier's 1963 paper cites the *Meno* and the *Theaetetus* as sources of the "justified true belief" analysis. That attribution partly anachronizes Plato, for whom the "tie" is explanatory understanding rather than evidential justification.
* **Knowledge and opinion in Republic V (476d–480a):** Socrates addresses the "lovers of sights and sounds," who recognize many beautiful things but deny Beauty itself. He argues as follows:
  1. Capacities (*dunameis*) are distinguished by what they are "set over" and what they accomplish.
  2. Knowledge is infallible (*anamartēton*, 477e); opinion is fallible. So they are different capacities with different objects.
  3. Knowledge is set over what is, ignorance over what is not, and opinion over what both is and is not.
  4. The many beautiful things both are and are not beautiful.
  5. Therefore the sight-lovers have only opinion, and knowledge is set over the Forms.
* **The two-worlds debate:**
  * *Traditional "two-worlds" reading:* Knowledge and belief have disjoint sets of objects. There is no knowledge of sensibles and no belief about Forms.
  * *Fine's reading:* "What is" should be read veridically ("what is true"), and the argument is addressed to the sight-lovers using premises they would accept. On this view Plato claims only that knowledge requires grasping Forms. It does not follow that sensibles cannot be known, or that a person cannot have beliefs about Forms.
  
  The choice matters for the *Republic*'s politics: on the two-worlds reading, the philosopher-ruler's knowledge seems to have no direct bearing on the sensible city.
* **The Theaetetus:** This aporetic late-middle dialogue examines three definitions of knowledge and rejects all three.
  * *Knowledge is perception (151e–186e):*
    * Socrates links the definition to Protagoras' doctrine that "a human being is the measure of all things" and to a Heraclitean theory of universal flux and perception.
    * *Self-refutation (170a–171c):* If every judgment is true for the person who makes it, then the judgment of those who believe Protagoras false is also true. Protagoras must concede his opponents' view. Myles Burnyeat argued that the argument is sound once the relativist's "true for x" is taken seriously.
    * *Expertise (178a–179b):* Judgments about the future (what will be healthful or profitable) show that some people are better judges than others.
    * *Radical flux (181c–183b):* Language becomes impossible if everything changes in every respect.
    * *The decisive argument (184b–186e):* The senses each perceive their proper objects (color, sound). Common features such as being, sameness, difference, likeness, and number are grasped by the soul "by itself," through reasoning about perceptions. Knowledge requires grasping being and truth, and perception does not reach being. So perception is not knowledge. John Cooper and Burnyeat read this as a distinction between sensory awareness and judgment. It is often regarded as one of Plato's most important contributions to philosophy of mind.
  * *Knowledge is true judgment (187a–201c):*
    * A long digression on the possibility of false judgment introduces the wax tablet (191c–195b) and the aviary (197c–200c). Both fail to explain errors in pure thought, such as mistaking eleven for twelve.
    * The definition is refuted by the jury case (201a–c). Jurors persuaded by rhetoric may form a true belief about a crime they did not witness, but they do not know it.
  * *Knowledge is true judgment with a logos (201c–210a):*
    * *The "dream theory":* Elements have no account and are unknowable; complexes are knowable through accounts.
    * *The syllable dilemma (203a–206b):* If a syllable is nothing but its letters, knowing it requires knowing them. If it is a single form distinct from its letters, it is as unknowable as an element. Experience of learning to read also suggests that elements are more knowable than complexes.
    * *Three senses of logos:* Socrates rejects each in turn. Logos as vocal expression is trivial. Logos as enumeration of elements is insufficient: Hesiod's "hundred timbers of a wagon," or knowing letters yet misspelling. Logos as the distinguishing mark (*diaphora*) is circular, since knowledge of the difference already requires knowledge.
* **Interpretation of the Theaetetus:** F. M. Cornford argued, from a unitarian standpoint, that the dialogue fails because it deliberately excludes the Forms. Gilbert Ryle, G. E. L. Owen, and John McDowell read it as an independent investigation in which Plato tests new epistemological ideas. Burnyeat argues that its structure is maieutic: it teaches by exhibiting the failures of plausible definitions.
* **Major objections:**
  * Recollection explains learning by positing prenatal learning. It either regresses or treats some knowledge as simply given, without explaining how the soul first acquired it.
  * Infallibility, as a criterion for knowledge, threatens to make knowledge either impossible or confined to necessary truths.
  * The sharp separation between knowledge and belief leaves unclear how knowledge of Forms bears on action in the sensible world.
* **Philosophical significance:** Plato formulated the fundamental problems of epistemology: the paradox of inquiry, the difference between knowledge and true belief, the role of perception in knowledge, and the structure of complex knowledge. Recollection anticipates rationalist innatism (Descartes, Leibniz) and debates about a priori knowledge. The *Theaetetus*'s distinction between perceiving and judging anticipates later work on the conceptual element in perception. The account of knowledge as explanatory understanding has been revived in contemporary discussions of understanding as an epistemic good.

**Ethics and Moral Philosophy**: `eudaimonism`
* **Central thesis:** The good life is the life of virtue (*aretē*). It is the happiest life (*eudaimonia*) because it consists in the proper order of the soul under reason. Ultimately, a life is good through knowledge of the Good. Plato preserves the Socratic link between virtue and knowledge but revises it in two ways. He recognizes non-rational motivation, and he grounds ethics in a metaphysics of the Good.
* **From Socratic intellectualism to the Republic:** The *Protagoras*, *Gorgias*, and *Meno* present the Socratic theses: virtue is knowledge; no one does wrong willingly; the virtues form a unity. The *Republic* retains the first in a modified form. Full virtue requires philosophical knowledge of the Good, which only philosophers possess. Plato also recognizes a "civic" or "popular" virtue (*dēmotikē aretē*), produced by habituation and correct belief without understanding (*Republic* 430c; *Phaedo* 82a–b). This is a major shift. Virtue can now exist without knowledge, but only in an incomplete, dependent form.
* **Thrasymachus' challenge (Republic I):** Thrasymachus claims that justice is the "advantage of the stronger" (338c), and further that it is "another's good" (343c). The just person is exploited; the perfectly unjust person, the tyrant, is happiest. Socrates offers several replies, the most important being the function (*ergon*) argument (352d–354a).
  1. The function of the soul is to deliberate, rule, and live.
  2. A thing performs its function well by its proper virtue.
  3. Justice is the virtue of the soul.
  4. Therefore the just soul lives well, and living well is being happy.
  
  The argument assumes what is in dispute: that justice is the soul's virtue. Book I ends by acknowledging that Socrates does not yet know what justice is.
* **The challenge of Glaucon and Adeimantus (Republic II):**
  * *The classification of goods (357b–d):* Some goods are valued only for themselves (harmless pleasures). Some are valued only for their consequences (medical treatment, money-making). Some are valued both ways (knowing, seeing, health). Socrates places justice in the third, finest class.
  * *Glaucon's case:* Justice originates in a compact among the weak not to harm one another (358e–359b). The Ring of Gyges suggests that anyone with impunity would act unjustly (359c–360d). Glaucon then compares the perfectly unjust person with a reputation for justice and the perfectly just person with a reputation for injustice, tortured and crucified (360e–362c).
  * *Adeimantus' complaint:* Parents and poets praise justice only for its rewards and reputation.
  * *The demand:* Socrates must show that justice, apart from all reputation and reward, is in itself good for the person who has it, and that the just person is happier.
* **Plato's answer:**
  * *Justice as psychic order:* Justice in the soul is each part "doing its own." Reason rules with wisdom; spirit supports reason; appetite obeys (441d–444a). Injustice is civil war (*stasis*) in the soul. Justice relates to the soul as health relates to the body (444c–e). Since no one would choose life with a diseased body, still less with a diseased soul.
  * *Book IX's three proofs:*
    1. *The tyrant's soul (576c–580c):* The tyrannical soul is enslaved by its worst part and is therefore the most miserable.
    2. *The judge of pleasures (580d–583a):* Each part of the soul has its own pleasures. Only the philosopher has experienced all three kinds and can judge by experience, wisdom, and argument. The philosopher judges philosophical pleasure best.
    3. *True pleasure (583b–587b):* Non-philosophical pleasures are mostly relief from pain or replenishment of a lack. They are "shadow-painted," mistaken for positive pleasures by contrast with pain. Only the pleasures of understanding are pure and true. Socrates whimsically computes that the king lives 729 times more pleasantly than the tyrant (587e).
* **Sachs's fallacy:** David Sachs argued that Plato's defense commits a fallacy of irrelevance. Glaucon asked about "vulgar" justice: not stealing, not breaking oaths, not committing adultery. Plato defines "Platonic" justice as psychic harmony. Plato must show both that psychic harmony entails vulgar justice and that vulgar justice requires psychic harmony.
  * *Plato's own reply:* Plato briefly argues that the harmonious person would not commit such acts (442e–443a), without a full argument.
  * *Vlastos and Annas:* Psychic order removes the motives (greed, ambition, fear) that lead to injustice.
  * *Kraut:* The philosopher's love of the Forms and desire to imitate their order generates just action toward others.
  
  The debate shows how far Plato's account depends on his moral psychology.
* **The philosopher's return to the cave:** The philosophers must be "compelled" to rule (519c–521b), even though contemplation is better for them. This seems to imply that justice can require a sacrifice of the agent's happiness, contradicting the eudaimonist thesis. The answer that "it is a just command given to just people" (520e) raises the question of whether justice is good for its possessor in every case.
  * *Irwin and Kraut:* The just person's love of the Forms extends to reproducing order in others, so ruling expresses, rather than sacrifices, their good.
  * *Other critics:* The tension is irreducible; Plato's eudaimonism admits an element of impartial obligation.
* **What "good in itself" means:** Plato's answer appeals to the effects of justice on the soul. Some critics (H. A. Prichard, M. B. Foster) objected that this makes justice valuable for its consequences after all. Defenders reply that a state of the soul is not an external consequence. Justice is valued for what it is in the soul, which is psychic health, as opposed to the rewards it brings.
* **The Philebus:** In this late dialogue Plato argues that neither a life of pleasure without intelligence nor a life of intelligence without pleasure is complete. The best life is a mixture, and what makes it good is measure (*metron*), proportion, beauty, and truth (64e–65a). The final ranking (66a–c) places measure first, then proportion and beauty, intelligence and wisdom, the sciences and arts, and only fifth the pure pleasures. The dialogue also develops a theory of false pleasures (36c–41a). The *Philebus* shows that late Plato did not abandon his ethics but integrated pleasure, as an ordered component, into a mathematically structured conception of the good.
* **Is the just person necessarily happier?** The *Republic* claims that the just person is happier in all circumstances. Plato's argument relies on two claims: the psychological thesis that disordered souls are wretched, and the evaluative thesis that the pleasures of reason are superior. The first has empirical plausibility in many cases but faces counterexamples of apparently contented wrongdoers. The second rests on the questionable premise that only someone who has experienced all kinds of pleasure can judge them, and that the philosopher alone has done so.
* **Philosophical significance:** The *Republic*'s challenge, "Why should I be just?", remains the standard formulation of the problem of the rational authority of morality. Plato's answer pioneers a distinctive strategy that Aristotle, the Stoics, and modern virtue ethicists develop: ground morality in a theory of the agent's well-being, understood as the excellent condition of a rational being, rather than in external sanctions or contracts. The social contract account he rejects, voiced by Glaucon, is equally influential as an early statement of contractarianism.

**Psychology and Theory of the Soul**: `soul–body dualism`, `psychological tripartition`
* **Central thesis:** The soul (*psychē*) is the principle of life and the seat of thought, desire, and character. In the *Phaedo* it is a separable entity akin to the Forms. In the *Republic* and *Phaedrus* it is complex, with three parts: reason, spirit, and appetite. Their order or disorder determines a person's character and happiness. In several dialogues Plato argues that the soul, or its rational part, is immortal.
* **The Phaedo:**
  * *Philosophy as practice for dying:* The body hinders knowledge, because perception is inaccurate and bodily desires distract (65a–67b). Philosophy, by separating the soul from the body as far as possible, is a "practice for dying" (64a, 67e).
  * *The cyclical argument (70c–72e):* Opposites come to be from opposites. Life comes from death as death from life. Without such a cycle, everything would end in death.
  * *The recollection argument (72e–77a):* It shows only that the soul existed before birth, not that it survives death.
  * *The affinity argument (78b–84b):* The soul resembles what is invisible, unchanging, and divine; the body resembles what is visible and perishable. Hence the soul is likely indestructible. The argument is explicitly probabilistic.
  * *Simmias's objection (85e–86d):* The soul may be an attunement (*harmonia*) of the body, like the tuning of a lyre, which perishes with the instrument. Socrates replies that an attunement cannot oppose its components, whereas the soul rules and resists the body (94b–e). Furthermore, the attunement theory is incompatible with recollection, which requires the soul to exist before the body.
  * *Cebes's objection (87b–88b):* Like a weaver who wears out many cloaks but eventually dies, the soul may outlast many bodies yet still perish.
  * *The final argument (102a–107b):* Some things essentially bring with them a Form and cannot admit its opposite. Fire brings heat and cannot admit cold; three brings oddness. The soul essentially brings life, so it cannot admit death; it is deathless (*athanatos*). Whatever is deathless is imperishable (*anōlethros*).
  * *Criticism of the final argument:* The step from "deathless" to "imperishable" (106a–e) is weak. Snow cannot admit heat and remain snow, but it can perish by melting. Similarly, the soul cannot be dead while it exists as soul, yet it might cease to exist. Plato secures the conclusion only by asserting that the deathless is also imperishable. Socrates himself says that the first hypotheses require further examination (107b).
* **The tripartite soul (Republic IV, 436a–441c):**
  1. *The principle of opposites (436b–c):* The same thing cannot do or undergo opposites in the same respect, in relation to the same thing, at the same time.
  2. *Appetite and reason:* A thirsty person may refuse to drink. Thirst as such is desire for drink, not for good drink (437d–439a). Since attraction to and repulsion from the same object cannot belong to the same part, the reasoning part (*logistikon*) is distinct from the appetitive part (*epithumētikon*).
  3. *Spirit and appetite:* Leontius was drawn to look at corpses and angry with himself for doing so (439e–440a). Spirit (*thumoeidēs*) can oppose appetite, so it is distinct from appetite.
  4. *Spirit and reason:* Children and animals show spirit without reason. In Homer, Odysseus' reason rebukes his anger (441b–c). So spirit is also distinct from reason.
* **The nature of the "parts":** Are they faculties, motivational sources, or quasi-agents with their own beliefs and desires? Plato's language often personifies them: they "agree," "persuade," "rule." This invites a homunculus objection: each part would itself be a complex agent requiring further division. Julia Annas, Christopher Bobonich, and Hendrik Lorenz discuss whether the non-rational parts have cognitive states. Lorenz argues that appetite operates with representations derived from perception and imagination, not with beliefs.
* **Tripartition and akrasia:** Tripartition allows real psychological conflict. Socrates had denied akrasia. Plato now explains how a person can act contrary to rational judgment: when appetite or spirit overpowers reason. Whether Plato admits "strict" akrasia—action contrary to *knowledge*—is disputed. A soul whose reason has knowledge and rules securely is perhaps immune.
* **Tripartition and immortality:** The relation between the two is unstable across the dialogues.
  * *Republic X (611b–612a):* The soul in its true nature may be simple. In embodied life it is encrusted like the sea-god Glaucus with shells and seaweed.
  * *Timaeus (69c–72d):* Only reason is immortal. Spirit and appetite are "mortal kinds of soul," located in the chest and the belly.
  * *Phaedrus (246a–254e):* The chariot image of a charioteer with a good and a bad horse attributes complexity even to the disembodied soul and to the gods' souls.
  
  These divergences suggest that Plato did not hold a single fixed view.
* **Self-motion:** The *Phaedrus* (245c–246a) argues that soul is self-moving. What moves itself is the source of motion for everything else and is therefore ungenerated and indestructible. The *Laws* X (893b–896c) develops this into an argument that soul is prior to body and the cause of cosmic motion.
* **Soul and character:** The order of the soul determines character type. Wisdom is the knowledge in reason of what is good for each part and the whole. Courage is spirit's preservation of reason's beliefs about what is to be feared. Temperance is the agreement among the parts about which should rule. Justice is each part doing its own. The degenerate character types of Books VIII–IX are defined by which part, or which kind of appetite, rules: timocratic (spirit), oligarchic (necessary appetites), democratic (all appetites as equals), tyrannical (lawless appetites).
* **Major objections:**
  * The argument for parts relies on a principle of opposites that may be too strong. It seems to multiply parts whenever any desires conflict.
  * The arguments for immortality are individually weak, and Plato's own characters acknowledge their limitations.
  * The relation between the separable soul of the *Phaedo* and the divided soul of the *Republic* remains unresolved.
* **Philosophical significance:** Tripartition is the first systematic theory of psychological conflict and of the non-rational sources of action. It shaped Aristotle's distinction between rational and non-rational parts, the Stoic counter-thesis of a unified rational soul, and later accounts ranging from Christian moral psychology to Freud's structural model, a comparison Freud himself drew. The resemblance with Freud is analogical, not genealogical. Plato's arguments for immortality, especially the final argument of the *Phaedo*, set the agenda for later debates about the soul as a substance distinct from the body.

**Political Philosophy and Education**: `political perfectionism`
* **Central thesis:** The purpose of the political community is to make citizens as good as possible. The best city is ruled by those who know the Good: the philosophers. In the *Republic*, justice in the city consists in each class performing its own function. This mirrors justice in the soul. Plato's later political works qualify the ideal. The *Statesman* and *Laws* assign the rule of law a central place as a second-best substitute for rule by knowledge.
* **The city-soul analogy:** Socrates proposes to study justice in the city as "larger letters" before studying it in the individual (368c–369a).
  * *The first city:* A city arises from mutual need and the principle of specialization: one person, one function, according to nature (370a–c). The "healthy" first city, which Glaucon calls a "city of pigs" (372d), is followed by a "luxurious" city. This requires an army and therefore guardians.
  * *The three classes:* Rulers, auxiliaries, and producers correspond to reason, spirit, and appetite. Justice is each class doing its own work (433a–b).
  * *Williams's objection:* Bernard Williams argued that the analogy is internally inconsistent. If a city is just because its parts are just, and the producing class is just by being dominated by appetite, then most citizens must have appetite-dominated souls. This is incompatible with their being just persons in the Platonic sense. Jonathan Lear replies that the analogy is not a whole-part rule but a dynamic of internalization and externalization: citizens internalize cultural forms, and rulers externalize their psychic order in institutions.
* **The three "waves" (Republic V):**
  1. *Women:* Women of the guardian class are to share education and rule (451c–457b). Plato's reason is that sex is as irrelevant to ruling as baldness is to shoemaking. He also holds that women are, on average, weaker.
  2. *Abolition of private families among the guardians (457c–466d):* Communal marriage and child-rearing are regulated by rigged lotteries and eugenic selection, to eliminate private interest.
  3. *Philosopher-kings:* "Until philosophers rule as kings, or those now called kings genuinely philosophize, there will be no end to the troubles of cities" (473c–e).
* **Why philosophers should rule:** Several lines of argument support the claim.
  * *Knowledge:* Ruling requires knowledge of the good of the city, which only philosophers have. The ship of state image (488a–489a) contrasts the true navigator, who studies the stars and seasons, with sailors who flatter the shipowner, the demos, to gain the helm.
  * *Paradigm:* Philosophers can use the Forms as a paradigm in shaping laws and characters (500d–501c).
  * *Motivation:* Philosophers do not want to rule. Their reluctance makes them incorruptible, since "the city where those who are to rule are least eager is necessarily governed best" (520d).
  * *Education:* Rulers are selected and tested over decades for both intellect and character.
* **Ideology and censorship:** The rulers may use "noble lies" for the city's benefit. The myth of the metals (414b–415d) holds that citizens are born with gold, silver, or bronze and iron in their souls, legitimating class division as natural. Poetry is censored and regulated (Books II–III and X). The rulers alone determine marriage pairings.
