* **Sophistical refutations:** A fallacy is an argument that appears to be a refutation or deduction but is not. Aristotle classifies thirteen.
  * *Six dependent on language:* homonymy, amphiboly, combination, division, accent, and form of expression.
  * *Seven independent of language:* accident, *secundum quid* (confusing a qualified with an unqualified statement), *ignoratio elenchi*, begging the question, consequent, false cause, and many questions.
  
  This was the first systematic theory of fallacies. It shaped medieval logic and modern informal logic.
* **Major objections:** Syllogistic logic is monadic and cannot represent relations or nested quantification. Augustus De Morgan's example "if all horses are animals, all heads of horses are heads of animals" cannot be proved in it. The traditional existential import of A propositions makes some conversions invalid for empty terms. Frege's quantificational logic absorbed and superseded the syllogistic. Within its domain, however, Aristotle's system is sound and complete for the inferences it addresses, as shown by Corcoran's and Smiley's reconstructions.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle discovered several basic ideas of logic:
  * Formal validity, by using schematic letters for terms.
  * Proof of invalidity by counterexample.
  * Systematic reduction of valid forms to a small set of basic ones.
  * The distinction between valid inference and true premises.
  * The relation between modality and temporality.
  
  Kant claimed that logic had not been able to advance a step beyond Aristotle. This was false, but it shows how thoroughly the Aristotelian system dominated logic for over two thousand years.

**Categories and the Structure of Predication**: `substance ontology`
* **Central thesis:** Things that are fall into a small number of irreducible highest kinds, the categories. The first and most basic of them is substance (*ousia*). Individual substances—this human, this horse—are primary because everything else depends on them for its existence. The structure of predication, saying something of something, both reflects and helps reveal this ontological order.
* **The ten categories (Categories 4, 1b25–2a4):** Substance (human, horse), quantity (two cubits long), quality (white, grammatical), relation (double, larger), place (in the Lyceum), time (yesterday), position (lying, sitting), having (shod, armed), doing (cutting), and being affected (being cut). Elsewhere Aristotle often lists fewer. The list seems to be a working classification, not a deduced system.
* **The four-fold division (Categories 2, 1a20–b9):** Two relations organize all beings: being *said of* a subject, which is roughly essential predication of a universal; and being *in* a subject, which is roughly inherence of a non-substantial feature, "not as a part, and incapable of existing separately from what it is in."
  * *Said of a subject but not in one:* universal substances, such as human being.
  * *In a subject but not said of one:* individual non-substances, such as "this individual grammatical knowledge" or "this individual white."
  * *Both said of and in a subject:* universal non-substances, such as knowledge or color.
  * *Neither:* individual substances, such as this human being.
* **Primary and secondary substances:** Individual substances are "primary substances." Their species and genera are "secondary substances" (*Categories* 5). Primary substances are the subjects of everything else: "if primary substances did not exist, it would be impossible for anything else to exist" (2b5–6). Substances have the following marks:
  * They are not in a subject.
  * They are predicated synonymously, giving their name and definition.
  * They signify "this something" (*tode ti*).
  * They have no contrary and do not admit of more or less.
  * Most distinctively, a substance remains numerically one while receiving contraries (4a10–11). The same human becomes pale and then dark.
* **Non-substantial particulars:** What exactly is "this white"? Two readings dominate.
  * *Ackrill's traditional reading:* The individual accidents are non-recurrent particulars. They are dependent on their subjects and cannot be shared, much like modern tropes.
  * *Owen's reading:* "This white" is a maximally determinate shade, which could recur in many subjects. "Not in a subject" then means only that it is not predicable of something lower in its own category.
  
  The issue affects whether the Categories ontology includes tropes. More broadly, it affects how Aristotle understands the dependence of non-substances on substances.
* **Language and ontology:** The categories are approached through predication: kinds of answers to questions about a subject ("what is it?", "how much?", "what is it like?"). Several readings explain how they were derived.
  * *Grammatical (Trendelenburg):* The categories derive from parts of speech.
  * *Interrogative (Ackrill):* They derive from the irreducible kinds of question one can ask about a subject.
  * *Ontological:* Aristotle classifies beings themselves, using language as evidence.
  
  *On Interpretation* 1 articulates a semantic triangle. Spoken sounds are symbols of "affections of the soul" (thoughts). These differ across languages, but the affections are the same for all humans and are likenesses of things. Aristotle thus treats language as conventional while holding that meaning is grounded in thought and reality.
* **Homonymy and synonymy:** *Categories* 1 distinguishes three relations.
  * *Homonymy:* Same name, different definition. "Animal" applies both to a human and to a picture of one.
  * *Synonymy:* Same name and same definition.
  * *Paronymy:* Derivative naming, as "grammarian" comes from "grammar."
  
  Homonymy is a philosophical tool throughout Aristotle's work. He uses it to diagnose apparent paradoxes and, above all, to show that "being" is not a genus but said "in many ways."
* **Major objections:** The list of categories lacks a principled derivation, a charge pressed by Kant and others. The status of non-substantial particulars is unclear. The Categories ontology, in which the matter-form composite individual is the primary substance, seems to conflict with *Metaphysics* Z, where form is called primary substance (see Hylomorphism below).
* **Philosophical significance:** The categories became the standard framework of ontological classification in late antiquity and the Middle Ages, transmitted largely through Porphyry's *Isagoge*. The distinction between substances and their dependent features, the concept of category mistakes (Gilbert Ryle's revival), and the analysis of predication remain central to metaphysics. Contemporary debates about tropes, substrata, and fundamental ontological categories reformulate questions first raised here.

**Metaphysics and the Science of Being**: `essentialism`
* **Central thesis:** There is a science—"first philosophy," sometimes "theology" or "wisdom"—that studies being *qua* being, the most general principles of everything that is. Because "being" is said in many ways but always with reference to substance, this science is primarily a study of substance. Aristotle asks what substance is. His answer is form or essence (*to ti ēn einai*), and above all the pure actuality of the divine intellect.
* **Being qua being and focal meaning:** "Being" is not a single genus, since a genus cannot be predicated of its differentiae. Nor is it merely homonymous. It is said *pros hen*, in relation to one thing (*Metaphysics* Γ.2, 1003a33–b10). All healthy things are so called with reference to health: a diet produces it, a complexion signals it, a body has it. In the same way, all beings are so called by reference to substance: qualities are qualities of substances, quantities are quantities of substances, and so on. G. E. L. Owen called this "focal meaning." On this basis a single science of being is possible, focused on substance and its principles.
* **The principle of non-contradiction (Γ.3–6):** The PNC holds that "it is impossible for the same thing to belong and not to belong to the same thing at the same time and in the same respect" (1005b19–20). It is the "firmest principle of all." It cannot be demonstrated, since every demonstration presupposes it. It can, however, be defended by "elenctic demonstration" (1006a11–28). An opponent who denies the PNC must say something significant, and to say something significant one must signify one determinate thing. Doing so presupposes that what is signified is not also its negation. Γ.5–6 connects denial of the PNC to Protagorean relativism and Heraclitean flux.
  * *Criticism:* Łukasiewicz argued that Aristotle's defenses are inconclusive and conflate ontological, logical, and psychological versions of the principle.
  * *Contemporary relevance:* Debates over dialetheism (Graham Priest) revisit the question whether the PNC admits rational defense.
* **The two conceptions of first philosophy:** In *Metaphysics* E.1, Aristotle distinguishes three theoretical sciences: physics, about separable but changeable things; mathematics, about unchangeable but not separable things; and theology, about separable and unchangeable things. If there is an unchangeable substance, the science of it is "first," and "universal because first" (1026a23–32). This creates an apparent tension. Is metaphysics general ontology, the study of being *qua* being, or special theology, the study of the highest being? Paul Natorp and Werner Jaeger saw this as evidence of different stages of composition. Günther Patzig and Michael Frede reconcile the two. On their reading, divine substance is the paradigm of substance and therefore the focal point for understanding all substance; theology is universal because it studies the primary instance of being.
* **The search for substance (Metaphysics Z):** Z.1 asserts the priority of substance in definition, knowledge, and time, and restates the ancient question "what is being?" as "what is substance?" Z.3 lists four candidates: essence, universal, genus, and the underlying subject (*hupokeimenon*).
  * *The subject:* Stripping away all properties from a thing would leave a mere matter that is "neither a particular thing nor a quantity nor anything else." Yet substance must be separable (*chōriston*) and a "this something" (*tode ti*). So matter cannot be substance, and form and the composite are better candidates.
  * *Essence (Z.4–6):* Essence belongs primarily to substances. For primary items, a thing and its essence are the same (Z.6). This identity blocks a regress and grounds the knowability of substances.
  * *Generation (Z.7–9):* Form is neither generated nor destroyed. What comes to be is the composite, formed by a pre-existing form ("a human begets a human").
  * *Definition (Z.10–12):* Aristotle asks which parts of a thing belong in its definition, and how a definition can be one rather than many.
  * *Universals (Z.13–16):* No universal is substance. The substance of each thing is peculiar to it, whereas a universal is common to many (1038b8–15). This argument also excludes genera and Platonic Forms.
  * *A new beginning (Z.17):* Substance is a cause (*aitia*). To ask "why are these bricks and stones a house?" is to ask for the form that makes this matter a particular kind of thing. A syllable is not merely its letters but something further: a principle, not an element.
* **The tension between Z.13 and form as substance:** If form is substance, and no universal is substance, form must be particular. Yet definitions and scientific knowledge are of universals (M.10). This leads to the debate over particular versus universal forms (see Hylomorphism below). Aristotle's own response in M.10 is that knowledge of the universal is actual knowledge potentially of particulars. On this reading the conflict is less sharp than it seems.
* **Unity:** Book Ι analyzes the one (*to hen*) in parallel with being. It is said in many ways: continuity, wholeness, the unity of the individual, the unity of the universal. It functions primarily as a measure. One and being are coextensive and are predicated in parallel.
* **The divine substance (Metaphysics Λ):** Λ distinguishes three kinds of substance: sensible and perishable; sensible and eternal (the heavens); and non-sensible and unchangeable. It then argues for an eternal, unmoved substance that is pure actuality (discussed under Natural Philosophy below). Its activity is thinking of itself, "thinking of thinking" (*noēsis noēseōs*, Λ.9, 1074b34–35).
* **Major objections:** The *Metaphysics* is a collection of treatises, not a unified book. Its central question—what is substance?—receives answers that are hard to reconcile:
  * The composite (*Categories*, parts of Z).
  * Form (Z.7, Z.11, Z.17).
  * The divine intellect as the paradigm (Λ).
  
  The relation between form, essence, and universality remains disputed. Aristotle's aporetic method, set out in Book B, makes the work as much a record of investigation as a doctrinal treatise.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle invented metaphysics as a discipline: the systematic study of being, substance, essence, and the principles of explanation. Essentialism, the distinction between substance and attribute, and the analysis of identity and unity have been central topics ever since. Kripke-style modal essentialism and the contemporary revival of neo-Aristotelian metaphysics have renewed interest in Aristotle's positions. These revivals are analogical, not continuous: Aristotle's essence is explanatory and definitional, not primarily a matter of necessity across possible worlds.

**Hylomorphism and the Theory of Substance**: `hylomorphism`
* **Central thesis:** Ordinary substances—organisms above all—are composites of matter (*hulē*) and form (*morphē*, *eidos*). Matter is what a thing is made of, the potentiality for being that kind of thing. Form is what makes the matter into the thing it is: the actuality, essence, and organizing principle. Form has ontological and explanatory priority. A thing is what it is because of its form, and a thing's form is its essence.
* **Origins in the analysis of change:** *Physics* I.7 argues that every change requires three principles.
  * *A substrate* that persists: the bronze, the human being.
  * *A form* that is acquired: the statue's shape, musical knowledge.
  * *A privation*: the prior absence of the form.
  
  Applied to substantial generation, the matter of a statue (bronze) persists while it acquires a new form. Aristotle extends the model to the generation of organisms, where the matter is menstrual blood and the form is provided by the father. Hylomorphism therefore began as a theory of change. *Metaphysics* Z–H transformed it into a theory of the constitution of substances.
* **Relative and hierarchical matter:** "Matter is a relative term: to each form there corresponds a special matter" (*Physics* II.2, 194b9). There is a hierarchy of matter. The elements are the matter of the homoeomerous tissues (flesh, bone, blood), which are the matter of organs (heart, hand), which are the matter of the organism. Each level is matter relative to the level above and a composite of matter and form relative to the level below. Whether the hierarchy bottoms out in "prime matter"—a substrate with no properties of its own, underlying elemental transformation—is disputed.
  * *The traditional view:* Prime matter is attributed to Aristotle, mainly on the basis of Z.3 and the generation of the elements in *On Generation and Corruption*.
  * *Critics:* H. R. King, William Charlton, and Mary Louise Gill have argued it is not Aristotle's doctrine. On their reading, elemental change is explained by the persistence of the contrary qualities hot, cold, wet, and dry.
* **Categories versus Metaphysics Z on primary substance:** In the *Categories*, the individual composite is the primary substance. In *Metaphysics* Z, form is called "primary substance" (Z.7, 1032b1–2; Z.11, 1037a5, where the soul is the primary substance).
  * *Developmental readings:* Daniel Graham's "two systems" view holds that the *Categories* reflects an earlier, pre-hylomorphic ontology.
  * *Compatibilist readings:* The *Categories* identifies which items are basic subjects of predication. Z identifies what makes them substances: their substance-of, which is form. The individual composite remains a substance; form is its primary principle.
* **The priority of form:** Form has priority in three respects.
  * *Explanatory:* It is the cause of the matter's being a determinate thing.
  * *In definition:* Definition states form, not matter, except where form is necessarily realized in certain kinds of matter. A snub nose is concavity in a nose.
  * *As actuality:* Form is the actuality of which matter is the potentiality.
  
  The functional character of form is shown by the homonymy principle. A severed hand or a dead eye is a hand or an eye only homonymously, because it can no longer perform its function (*Metaphysics* Z.10, 1035b23–25; *Politics* I.2, 1253a20–25; *De Anima* II.1, 412b20–22). Matter is what it is only in relation to the form it serves.
* **Ackrill's problem:** The homonymy principle generates a difficulty. Matter is supposed to be the potentiality for the form, something that could exist without it. Yet the organic body exists *as* that body only when ensouled: a dead body is a body only homonymously. If the proximate matter of an organism is necessarily enformed, in what sense is it potentially alive rather than actually so? Several replies have been offered.
  * *Two levels of matter:* Distinguish proximate matter, the functional organic body, which is necessarily enformed, from remote matter, the tissues and elements, which survive death.
  * *Functional definition:* The body's potentiality is defined by its form-determined function.
  * *Unity of the composite:* Matter and form are not two components but aspects of a single unified substance (*Metaphysics* H.6).
* **The unity of the composite (Metaphysics H.6):** Why is a substance one thing rather than a heap of matter plus form? Aristotle's answer: "the proximate matter and the form are one and the same thing, the one potentially, the other actually" (1045b17–19). The question "what makes them one?" is therefore misplaced. Being potentially the form is just what it is for the matter to be capable of being unified by it. This answer explains why definitions of composite substances, though they have parts (genus and differentia), can be unified.
* **Individuation:** What makes Socrates and Callias two individuals of the same species?
  * *Matter individuates:* Aristotle says they are different "because of their matter, but the same in form, for the form is indivisible" (*Metaphysics* Z.8, 1034a5–8). This suggests that form is shared by members of a species and matter accounts for plurality. The tradition developed this view through Aquinas's *materia signata*, matter designated by quantity.
  * *Particular forms:* *Metaphysics* Λ.5 (1071a27–29) states that "your matter and form and moving cause are different from mine, though the same in universal account." Michael Frede and Günther Patzig argued that Aristotelian forms are particular, so each individual has its own form. Others, such as Michael Woods and Alan Code, hold that the form is the species form.
  
  The choice affects how Aristotle's two claims—that form is substance and that no universal is substance—can be made consistent.
* **Philosophical significance:** Hylomorphism is Aristotle's most distinctive theory of what things are. It avoids Platonic dualism, because forms are not separate but immanent in things. It also avoids reductive materialism, because things are not just their constituents but organized wholes. The theory has been revived in contemporary metaphysics as an account of the part-whole structure of material objects (Kit Fine, Kathrin Koslicki, Mark Johnston). It is also used in philosophy of mind and biology as a "third way" between substance dualism and eliminativism.

**Causation and Explanation**: `natural teleology`
* **Central thesis:** To have knowledge of something is to grasp its cause (*aitia*): the answer to the question "why?" (*dia ti*). There are four types of cause (*Physics* II.3, 194b16–195a3; *Metaphysics* Δ.2), corresponding to four kinds of answer:
  * *Material:* "that from which, as a constituent, something comes to be," as the bronze of a statue.
  * *Formal:* the form or pattern, "the account of the essence," as the shape of the statue or the ratio 2:1 of the octave.
  * *Efficient:* "the primary source of change or rest," as the sculptor, the father, or the adviser.
  * *Final:* "that for the sake of which," the end (*telos*), as health is the end of walking.
* **Key concepts:**
  * *Aitia:* The Greek word is closer to "explanatory factor" or "reason why" than to the modern notion of an efficient event-cause. The four causes are therefore four kinds of explanation, not four kinds of forces.
  * *Coincidence of causes:* In natural generation, the formal, efficient, and final causes often "come together into one" (*Physics* II.7, 198a24–27). A human begets a human: the father supplies the efficient cause and has the same form as the offspring, and the form realized in the mature organism is the end of the developmental process.
  * *Hypothetical necessity:* Necessity in nature is mostly conditional (*Physics* II.9; *Parts of Animals* I.1). If a saw is to cut, it must be made of iron. If a house is to exist, foundations must be laid. Matter is necessary given the end, not the cause of the end.
* **History of causes:** *Metaphysics* A.3–10 reviews earlier philosophers to show that none recognized more than these four causes and that each grasped them only partially. The material monists (Thales, Anaximenes) recognized only the material cause. Empedocles and Anaxagoras introduced efficient causes (Love and Strife, Mind). Plato used the formal and material causes but neglected the efficient and final ones. Aristotle's use of predecessors as dialectical evidence makes him the first historian of philosophy, though his history is shaped by his own framework.
* **The argument for natural teleology (Physics II.8):** Aristotle considers an Empedoclean alternative. Zeus rains not in order to make grain grow, but because evaporated water cools and falls by necessity. Perhaps animal parts also arose by chance, and those that happened to be well suited survived. Aristotle's argument against this runs:
  1. Natural things come about always or for the most part in the same way. Teeth regularly grow sharp in front for tearing and broad at the back for grinding.
  2. Chance events, by contrast, are rare and irregular.
  3. Hence natural regularities that look purposive cannot be due to chance.
  4. The only remaining option is that they come about for the sake of something.
  
  He adds an analogy: "as in art, so in nature." The teleology of nature is like art without deliberation: "art does not deliberate" (199b28).
* **The rain passage:** It is debated whether Aristotle accepts that rain falls for the sake of crops.
  * *Sedley's reading:* Aristotle accepts a cosmic, even anthropocentric, teleology. Rain falls for the growth of crops, and plants exist for animals and animals for humans (*Politics* I.8, 1256b15–22).
  * *Majority reading (Wieland, Cooper, Gotthelf, Lennox):* Aristotelian teleology is primarily organismic. Final causes explain the parts and processes of living things by their contribution to the life of the organism. Rain is a case of material necessity.
* **The irreducibility of final causes:** Why are final causes necessary? Allan Gotthelf argued that for Aristotle, the development of an organism is the actualization of an "irreducible potential for form." Neither material nor elemental processes, operating by their own natures, could explain the organized result. They are directed by the form, which is transmitted through the father's semen as a set of formal "movements." John Cooper emphasized the regularity argument: material causes alone cannot explain the persistent reproduction of functionally organized species. In either form, the argument rests on a claim about the explanatory insufficiency of material causes. That claim is undermined, though not trivially, by natural selection, which explains functional organization without final causes in Aristotle's sense.
* **Teleology without design:** Aristotelian natural teleology involves no designer and no intention. Ends are immanent in natures, the internal principles of motion of living things. This contrasts with Plato's *Timaeus*, where a divine craftsman imposes order. The unmoved mover is a final cause of celestial motion, not a designer of organisms.
* **Major objections:**
  * Early modern philosophers (Bacon, Descartes, Spinoza) rejected final causes as unscientific or anthropomorphic.
  * Darwinian evolution offers an alternative explanation of functional organization. It is in fact similar to the Empedoclean view Aristotle rejected, except that it explains regularity by cumulative selection rather than single chance events.
  * Aristotle's distinction between what happens "always or for the most part" and chance is not sufficient to establish teleology.
* **Contemporary relevance:** Biology still uses functional explanations ("the heart is for pumping blood"). Modern accounts, such as the etiological theory of function (Larry Wright, Ruth Millikan), give a naturalistic grounding to the explanatory role Aristotle assigned to final causes. Contemporary "neo-Aristotelian" philosophers of biology defend the irreducibility of organismic organization. These positions do not depend on Aristotle's argument against Empedocles.
* **Philosophical significance:** The four causes provided the framework of explanation for science and philosophy until the seventeenth century. The analysis of explanation as answering "why?" questions anticipates modern philosophy of science, in which explanation is understood as relative to kinds of why-questions. The distinction between conditional and simple necessity remains a useful tool for analyzing how material constraints relate to functional explanation.

**Potentiality, Actuality, and Change**
* **Central thesis:** Change is real and intelligible. It does not violate the Eleatic principle that nothing comes from what is not. In change, something that is *potentially* F becomes *actually* F. The distinction between potentiality (*dunamis*) and actuality (*energeia*, *entelecheia*) is Aristotle's fundamental tool for analyzing change, substance, life, and the hierarchy of being.
* **The Eleatic problem (Physics I.8, 191a23–b34):** Parmenides argued that coming to be is impossible. A thing cannot come to be from what is, since it already is. Nor from what is not, since nothing comes from nothing. Aristotle replies in two ways.
  * *Qualified not-being:* The musical man comes to be from what is not musical—not from absolute not-being, but from a substrate (the man) characterized by a privation (non-musicality). He comes to be from not-being *per accidens*, and from being in a qualified sense.
  * *Potential being:* The same thing can be both actually not-F and potentially F. Change is a transition between them, not a passage from nothing to something.
* **The definition of motion (Physics III.1, 201a10–11):** Motion (*kinēsis*) is "the actuality of what is potentially, as such." Building is the actuality of the buildable *qua* buildable. The completed house is not the actuality of the buildable as buildable, since it is no longer buildable.
  * *Ross's reading:* "Actuality" means "actualization," the process of becoming actual.
  * *Kosman's reading:* It is the actuality of a potentiality *as* potentiality, the condition of being in the process of realizing it. Motion is then the paradoxical case of a potentiality that is actualized only as long as it remains unfulfilled.
* **Kinds of potentiality (Metaphysics Θ):**
  * *Potentiality for change* (*kata kinēsin*, Θ.1–5): the capacity to bring about change in another (active) or to be changed by another (passive).
  * *Ontological potentiality* (Θ.6–9): the potentiality of matter to be a certain kind of substance. Aristotle's examples are the Hermes potentially in the wood and the half-line potentially in the line.
  * *Rational and non-rational potentialities* (Θ.2, Θ.5): Rational potentialities, such as medicine, are two-way: they can produce opposite effects, health or illness. Decision or desire determines which is actualized. Non-rational potentialities, like fire's heating, are one-way and necessarily actualized when agent and patient meet.
* **Against the Megarians (Θ.3):** The Megarians held that a thing has a potentiality only when it is acting. Aristotle argues that this leads to absurdities. A builder would not be a builder when not building. Nothing would be perceptible when not perceived, which collapses into Protagorean relativism. Nothing could ever change, since what is standing could never sit. He defines the possible: something is possible if nothing impossible follows from supposing it actual (1047a24–26).
* **Energeia and kinēsis (Θ.6, 1048b18–35):** Aristotle distinguishes complete activities (*energeiai*) from incomplete motions (*kinēseis*) with a tense test. "At the same time one sees and has seen, thinks and has thought." But one is not at the same time building and having built. Seeing, thinking, and living well are complete at every moment; they contain their end. Building and walking are directed to an end outside themselves and are incomplete until finished. The distinction is important for Aristotle's theory of pleasure (*Nicomachean Ethics* X.4) and of happiness as activity.
  * *Ackrill* questioned the coherence of the test, since many verbs fit both patterns.
  * *Burnyeat* argued that the Θ.6 passage itself is a later insertion, not part of the original book, though the distinction appears elsewhere in Aristotle.
* **The priority of actuality (Θ.8):** Actuality is prior to potentiality in three ways.
  * *In definition:* To be capable of building is defined by building.
  * *In time, at the level of the species:* Though an individual is potentially human before being actually human, it comes from an actual human (the father).
  * *In substance:* What is later in generation is prior in form and end. Eternal things are prior to perishable things, and eternal things are fully actual.
  
  This principle grounds Aristotle's theology. The first principle must be pure actuality, without potentiality (Λ.6).
* **First and second actuality:** *De Anima* II.1 and II.5 distinguish three stages. Someone who can acquire knowledge is a learner (first potentiality). Someone who has acquired knowledge but is not using it has first actuality, which is also a second potentiality. Someone actively contemplating has second actuality. Transitions between these stages differ in kind. Learning is a change in the proper sense. The exercise of acquired knowledge is "a progression into itself and into actuality" (417b6–7), not a qualitative alteration.
* **Entelecheia and energeia:** Aristotle seems to have coined both words. *Energeia* derives from *ergon* (work, function) and means being at work. *Entelecheia* is related to *telos* and suggests being in a state of completion. Aristotle often uses them interchangeably, but *entelecheia* stresses the achieved end-state (as in the definition of the soul), while *energeia* stresses activity.
* **Major objections:** The concept of potentiality has been criticized as explanatorily empty. Molière's "dormitive virtue" of opium parodies the worry that potencies merely rename what they are supposed to explain. Defenders reply that Aristotelian potentialities are grounded in the natures of things and connected to their actualities by definition. Contemporary dispositionalism in metaphysics (Mumford, Bird) has revived the idea that causal powers are irreducible features of the world.
* **Philosophical significance:** The distinction between potentiality and actuality solves the Eleatic puzzle about change. It provides a framework for understanding both the unity of material substances and the hierarchy of life. It also underlies the distinction between processes and complete activities in action theory and linguistics, the aspectual classification of verbs, as Zeno Vendler acknowledged in acknowledging Aristotle's distinction.

**Natural Philosophy and Cosmology**: `plenism`, `finitism about the actual infinite`
* **Central thesis:** Nature (*phusis*) is an internal principle of motion and rest. The natural world consists of substances that move and change according to their natures. These include the four sublunary elements, with their natural motions toward their proper places, and the celestial bodies, made of a fifth element with eternal circular motion. Because motion is eternal, it requires a first, unmoved mover.
* **Nature versus art:** Natural things have "within themselves a principle of motion and of stationariness" (*Physics* II.1, 192b13–14). Artifacts have no such innate impulse to change, except insofar as they are made of natural materials. A bed, if it sprouted, would produce not a bed but wood. Nature in the primary sense is form rather than matter, because form is the end of natural development.
* **Elements and natural motion:** The four sublunary elements—earth, water, air, fire—move naturally in straight lines toward or away from the center of the cosmos, according to their heaviness or lightness. They rest in their natural places. Unnatural or forced motion requires an external mover. Since the stars move eternally in circles, Aristotle posits a fifth element, aether, whose natural motion is circular (*On the Heavens* I.2–3). This yields a sharp distinction between a perishable sublunary world and an imperishable celestial one.
* **Place (Physics IV.1–5):** Place is "the innermost motionless boundary of the containing body" (212a20–21). It is not matter, form, or an extension in its own right. The universe as a whole is not in a place.
* **The void (Physics IV.6–9):** Aristotle argues there is no void. In a void there would be no reason for a body to move in one direction rather than another. Speed would be infinite in the absence of resistance, since Aristotle holds that speed varies inversely with the density of the medium. And there would be no reason for a moving body to stop. Ironically, the last point resembles an anticipation of inertia, which Aristotle uses against the void rather than as a principle.
* **Time (Physics IV.10–14):** Time is "the number of motion with respect to before and after" (219b1–2). Time is not motion, since motions differ in speed while time is uniform. But time does not exist without motion. We perceive time when we perceive change. Aristotle raises a puzzle: would there be time without a soul to count? Since time is a number, and number requires a counter, perhaps time depends on soul. He leaves the question partly open.
* **Continuity and the infinite (Physics III.4–8, VI):** Magnitudes, motions, and times are continuous: infinitely divisible, but not composed of indivisible points. The infinite exists only potentially. A line can be divided without limit, but never infinitely divided in actuality; numbers can be increased without limit. There is no actual infinite (III.6).
* **Zeno's paradoxes:** Aristotle answers Zeno in two stages.
  * *Physics VI.2, VI.9:* If the distance is infinitely divisible, so is the time. A finite time contains infinitely many subdivisions corresponding to those of the distance.
  * *Physics VIII.8 (263a4–b9):* Aristotle recognizes that this answer is inadequate if the question is how one can complete infinitely many tasks. He refines it: a moving body does not actually traverse infinitely many points, because the points are only potentially present. A point becomes actual only if the motion stops or is marked there.
* **Modern comparison:** Modern mathematics, after Cantor, accepts actual infinities and treats continua as sets of points. Aristotle's potential infinite, however, anticipates the constructivist and intuitionist emphasis on the infinite as an open-ended process.
* **The unmoved mover:** *Physics* VIII and *Metaphysics* Λ.6–10 argue as follows.
  1. Motion and time are eternal: any first motion would require a prior change to start it, and time cannot have a beginning, since there would be a "before" it.
  2. Everything in motion is moved by something.
  3. An infinite regress of movers is impossible.
  4. Hence there is a first mover which is itself unmoved.
  5. To cause eternal motion, it must have no potentiality. If it had any, it could fail to act. So it is pure actuality and immaterial.
  6. Because it is immaterial and unmoved, it moves the outermost heaven not by pushing but "as being loved" (*hōs erōmenon*, Λ.7, 1072b3): as an object of desire and thought.
  7. Its activity is thought. Since it thinks what is best, it thinks itself: "its thinking is a thinking of thinking" (Λ.9).
  
  Λ.8 adds a separate unmoved mover for each celestial sphere: 47 or 55, depending on the astronomical theory adopted, Eudoxus's or Callippus's.
* **Interpretive issues about the mover:**
  * *Final or efficient cause:* Most interpreters take it as a final cause, an object of desire. Others, such as Enrico Berti, argue it is also an efficient cause.
  * *How can an object of love move the sphere?* The sphere seems to require a soul, which desires the mover.
  * *Does the mover know the world?* It knows only itself. This is a feature the tradition later struggled to reconcile with a provident God.
* **Major objections:** Aristotle's physics was overthrown in the scientific revolution.
  * Galileo and Newton replaced natural places with inertia and universal gravitation.
  * The celestial–sublunary distinction fell with the telescope and Newtonian mechanics.
  * Atomism and the void returned.
  
  Some insights survive. Aristotle's analysis of continuity and the potential infinite, his relational conception of time as dependent on change, and his treatment of motion as requiring a mover in the Aristotelian sense (a cause of each acceleration) remain philosophically valuable. They are not anticipations of modern physics.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle's natural philosophy dominated science until the seventeenth century, and its overthrow shaped the scientific revolution. The cosmological argument from motion to an unmoved mover became the basis of the first of Aquinas's "five ways." Aristotle's problems about time, the continuum, and the infinite remain central to the philosophy of physics and mathematics.

**Psychology and Philosophy of Mind**: `psychological hylomorphism`
* **Central thesis:** The soul (*psychē*) is not a separate entity inhabiting the body. It is the form of a living body: "the first actuality of a natural body that has life potentially," or "of a natural organic body" (*De Anima* II.1, 412a27–b6). To be alive is to have the capacities characteristic of living things. Different kinds of living things are defined by different, hierarchically nested capacities: nutrition, perception, locomotion, and thought.
* **The definition of soul and its consequences:**
  * *First actuality:* The soul is a capacity possessed even when not exercised, as one has knowledge while asleep. It is not an activity.
  * *Unity of soul and body:* "We need not ask whether soul and body are one, any more than whether the wax and the impression are one" (412b6–9). The question of how mind and body are connected, so central to Descartes, is on this view misconceived.
  * *Homonymy:* If an eye were an animal, sight would be its soul. A dead eye is an eye only homonymously (412b18–22).
* **The hierarchy of soul:** The nutritive soul (growth, nourishment, reproduction) belongs to all living things. The perceptual soul belongs to animals, together with desire, imagination, and in some cases locomotion. The rational soul belongs to humans. Each higher capacity presupposes the lower ones (II.3).
* **Perception (II.5–12; III.1–2):** Perception is a kind of alteration in which the sense receives the perceptible form "without the matter, as wax receives the imprint of a signet-ring without the iron or gold" (II.12, 424a17–24).
  * *Literalism (Richard Sorabji):* Perception involves a physiological change in the sense organ, so that the eye-jelly literally takes on color.
  * *Spiritualism (Myles Burnyeat):* The reception of form is not an ordinary physical change but a non-physical awareness. Burnyeat concluded that Aristotle's philosophy of mind is "no longer credible," because it presupposes a pre-modern physics in which matter is intrinsically receptive to awareness.
  
  The debate bears directly on whether Aristotle's psychology can be updated as a version of functionalism. Hilary Putnam and Martha Nussbaum argued that it can. Burnyeat argued that it cannot.
* **Objects of perception:**
  * *Special sensibles:* Color is perceived only by sight and sound only by hearing. Perception of these is "always true" or least liable to error.
  * *Common sensibles:* Motion, rest, number, shape, and size are perceived by more than one sense.
  * *Incidental perceptibles:* "The son of Diares" is perceived only incidentally, because the white thing one sees happens to be him.
  
  The "common sense" (III.1–2) is not a sixth sense. It is the unified perceptual capacity. It accounts for the perception of common sensibles, the discrimination between objects of different senses (white versus sweet), and the awareness that we perceive.
* **Imagination (phantasia, III.3):** *Phantasia* is "a movement produced by actual perception," the capacity responsible for after-images, dreams, and appearances. It underlies memory, animal desire, and all thought, since "the soul never thinks without a *phantasma*" (III.7, 431a16–17).
* **Intellect (nous, III.4–5):**
  * *III.4:* Intellect is "potentially" all things, the forms of things, and "actually nothing before it thinks." It has no bodily organ and is "unmixed" with the body. Otherwise its own nature would interfere with its reception of forms, just as a colored eye could not see all colors.
  * *III.5:* Aristotle distinguishes an intellect "that becomes all things" from one "that makes all things," like light, which makes potential colors actual. The latter is "separable, impassible, unmixed, being in essence actuality." It "alone is immortal and eternal."
* **Interpretations of the productive intellect:** The productive intellect is the most disputed passage in Aristotle's corpus.
  * *Alexander of Aphrodisias:* It is the divine intellect, the unmoved mover, acting from outside on individual human intellects. Individual human minds are mortal.
  * *Averroes:* There is a single "material" or potential intellect for all humans.
  * *Aquinas:* Each human soul has its own productive intellect. This preserves individual immortality.
  
  The passage is too brief to decide among these readings. Whatever the solution, intellect is Aristotle's one exception to hylomorphic dependence on a bodily organ.
* **Memory and recollection (On Memory and Recollection):** Memory belongs to the same part of the soul as perception and imagination. It is a *phantasma* treated "as a likeness of that of which it is a phantasma," with an awareness of past time (450a12–14; 451a14–17). Animals with time-perception can remember. Recollection (*anamnēsis*) is an active search that moves through associated images by similarity, contrast, or contiguity. Only humans can recollect, because recollection is a kind of reasoning. Aristotle thereby turns Plato's term into an empirical psychological concept and anticipates associationist psychology.
* **Major objections:**
  * The relation between the soul and its physical basis remains unclear. Whether psychological states are realized in, identical to, or merely accompanied by physiological processes is the literalism-spiritualism debate.
  * The immaterial intellect is in tension with hylomorphism.
  * Aristotle's physiology was erroneous: he located perception and thought in the heart, with the brain serving to cool the blood.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle's definition of soul as the form of the body offers an alternative to both dualism and reductive materialism. It treats mind as a matter of capacities and functional organization. Contemporary philosophers of mind cite it as an ancestor of functionalism, and contemporary hylomorphists defend it directly. The theory of perception as reception of form without matter shaped all medieval cognitive theory through the doctrine of intelligible species. The intellect problem shaped debates on immortality and individual identity for centuries.

**Biology and Philosophy of Nature**: `epigenesis`
* **Central thesis:** Living beings are the paradigm of natural substances. Their investigation requires systematic observation of the "facts" (*ta huparchonta*), followed by causal explanation. Biological structures should be explained mainly by their functions, together with the material conditions their functions require. Aristotle's biological works, roughly a quarter of the surviving corpus, are the first systematic science of life.
* **The corpus:**
  * *History of Animals:* Organizes data about over five hundred species: anatomy, habits, reproduction, habitats.
  * *Parts of Animals:* Explains the parts of animals by their causes.
  * *Generation of Animals:* Explains reproduction and inheritance.
  * *Movement of Animals* and *Progression of Animals:* Address locomotion.
* **Method (Parts of Animals I):** One should first collect the "that" (*hoti*), the phenomena, before seeking the "why" (*dioti*). Explanation should begin with the final cause: the account of what something is for. Material necessity is conditional on it (639b11–640a10). Aristotle defends the study of humble animals: "in all natural things there is something marvellous." He cites Heraclitus, who reportedly told visitors finding him warming himself at a stove that "here too there are gods" (645a4–23).
* **Classification:** Aristotle rejects classification by dichotomy, the Academic method of division (*Parts of Animals* I.2–3). It splits natural kinds (birds into land and water birds), cannot accommodate privative differentiae, and produces too many classes. Instead, animals should be grouped by many differentiae at once. Within a genus, kinds differ by "more and less," for example in beak length. Across genera, parts correspond by analogy: feather to scale, bone to fish-spine. Aristotle's classes include blooded and bloodless animals, roughly vertebrates and invertebrates. Within these he groups viviparous quadrupeds, birds, fishes, cephalopods, crustaceans, testaceans, and insects. David Balme argued that Aristotle's goal was not a taxonomy but an explanation of differentiae; on this view his biology is not "essentialist" in the Linnaean sense.
* **Functional explanation:** Teeth are sharp in front to cut and flat in back to grind. Ruminants have multiple stomachs to compensate for poor teeth. Elephants have trunks because they live in water and need a breathing tube that can also serve as a hand. Material necessity provides the conditions and often by-products. Bile, for example, is a residue without function. Explanation combines "for the sake of which" and "necessity."
* **Reproduction and heredity (Generation of Animals):**
  * *Contributions of each parent:* The male contributes the form and the efficient cause through the "movements" carried in the semen. The female contributes matter, the menstrual blood.
  * *Epigenesis:* Aristotle defends epigenesis against preformation. The embryo develops parts in sequence; they are not already present in miniature (II.1, 734a).
  * *Resemblance (IV.3):* When the father's movements dominate, the offspring resembles him. When they are resisted or "relapse" into movements of the mother or of ancestors, resemblance to them results.
  * *The female:* Aristotle's claim that the female is "as it were a deformed male" (II.3, 737a27–28) follows from his view that the female lacks the heat to "concoct" blood into semen. It is a clear example of empirical error combined with an androcentric theoretical bias.
* **Observation and error:** Aristotle reported observations later confirmed. Examples include the hectocotylized arm used in octopus reproduction, and the placenta-like attachment of the embryo in the smooth dogfish, confirmed by Johannes Müller in 1842. He also made errors. Some came from reliance on informants, some from theoretical commitments, such as the heart as the center of sensation and men having more teeth than women. Darwin, after reading Ogle's translation of *Parts of Animals*, wrote that Linnaeus and Cuvier had been his gods, but they were "mere school-boys to old Aristotle."
* **Major objections:**
  * Aristotle's species were fixed and eternal, which is inconsistent with evolution.
  * His teleology seems to presuppose that functional organization cannot arise from non-teleological processes.
  * His gendered biology is both empirically false and ideologically shaped.
  
  His methodological principles survive these criticisms: comparative anatomy, function as a guide to structure, systematic data collection before theory, and the explanatory unity of organisms.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle founded biology as a discipline. Through James Lennox, Allan Gotthelf, and others, his biology has become central to the interpretation of his whole philosophy. His metaphysics of substance, form, and teleology appears modeled on living organisms. In philosophy of biology, Aristotelian ideas about function, organization, and developmental potential have been revived in debates over reductionism, functional explanation, and evo-devo.

**Epistemology and Philosophy of Science**: `foundationalism`
* **Central thesis:** Scientific knowledge (*epistēmē*) is knowledge of why something is so, through its cause, together with the knowledge that it cannot be otherwise. Such knowledge is achieved through demonstration from first principles. The first principles themselves cannot be demonstrated. They are grasped through a process that begins with perception and culminates in intellect (*nous*).
* **Knowing simpliciter (Posterior Analytics I.2, 71b9–12):** "We think we know something without qualification when we think we know the cause on which the fact depends, as the cause of that fact and of no other, and further, that the fact could not be otherwise." Burnyeat argued that *epistēmē* is better rendered "understanding" than "knowledge." It is explanatory rather than primarily evidential, and the *Posterior Analytics* is a theory of explanation and understanding, not of justification against skepticism.
* **The regress argument (I.3):** If all knowledge required demonstration, there would be an infinite regress or a circle. Neither yields knowledge. Hence some principles must be known without demonstration. This is the earliest explicit statement of the foundationalist response to the epistemic regress.
* **Knowing that versus knowing why (I.13):** Two arguments can both be valid and have true premises, yet differ in explanatory order.
  * *Syllogism of the fact:* "Planets do not twinkle; what does not twinkle is near; therefore planets are near." This establishes only *that* the planets are near.
  * *Syllogism of the reasoned fact:* "Planets are near; what is near does not twinkle; therefore planets do not twinkle." This explains *why* they do not twinkle, since nearness is the cause.
  
  This distinction anticipates the asymmetry of explanation that the deductive-nomological model of explanation (Hempel) failed to capture. Sylvain Bromberger's flagpole example later made the same point.
* **First principles:**
  * *Axioms:* Common principles shared by many sciences, such as the PNC or "equals subtracted from equals leave equals."
  * *Theses:* Proper to each science. These include definitions and hypotheses, which posit existence: "there are units."
  * *No kind-crossing:* Each science studies one genus and uses principles proper to it (I.7). Demonstration cannot cross from one genus to another (*metabasis*). Arithmetic cannot prove geometrical theorems, except in subordinate sciences such as optics, which uses geometry.
* **Definition and demonstration (II.1–10):** Scientific inquiry asks four questions: *that* it is, *why* it is, *if* it is, and *what* it is. Aristotle argues that knowing what something is and knowing why it is are the same (II.2). An eclipse is the moon's loss of light due to the earth's interposition. The middle term of the demonstration—interposition of the earth—is the cause and appears in the full definition. Aristotle distinguishes nominal definitions, which say what a name signifies, from causal definitions, which explain what the thing is. The former guide the search for the latter. This view of definition as discovered through causal inquiry anticipates the modern idea of real definitions of natural kinds, as in water being H₂O.
* **The acquisition of first principles (Posterior Analytics II.19):** Aristotle describes a sequence: perception → memory → experience (*empeiria*) → universal, which is the principle of art and science. A universal comes to "rest in the soul," "as in a battle when a rout occurs, if one man makes a stand, another does and then another, until the original formation is restored" (100a12–13). The state that grasps principles is *nous*, intellect. It is more accurate than *epistēmē*, because it grasps principles rather than conclusions. Two main readings of this account compete.
  * *Rationalist:* *Nous* is an intuitive grasp of necessary truths, whose ground lies in intellectual insight.
  * *Empiricist:* *Nous* is the state of comprehension reached by induction from experience. It is not an independent faculty of intuition. David Bronstein and others have developed this line, sometimes with a role for dialectic.
* **Pedagogy versus discovery:** Jonathan Barnes argued that the *Posterior Analytics* describes not how science is discovered but how established knowledge is to be presented and taught. Aristotle's scientific practice, especially in biology, does not follow the demonstrative model. Others argue that demonstrative structure is the goal of inquiry, and that the biological works approximate it.
* **Experience and art (Metaphysics A.1):** People with experience know *that* something is the case. People with art (*technē*) know *why*. This is why the master craftsman is wiser than the manual worker, and why theoretical wisdom (*sophia*), knowledge of the first causes, is the highest. "All humans by nature desire to know" (980a21). The delight in perception is evidence of this desire.
* **Intellectual virtues (Nicomachean Ethics VI):** Aristotle distinguishes five states by which the soul grasps truth: *technē* (art), *epistēmē* (scientific knowledge), *phronēsis* (practical wisdom), *sophia* (theoretical wisdom), and *nous* (intellect). *Sophia* combines *nous* of principles with *epistēmē* of what follows from them. The distinction between theoretical wisdom, concerned with the necessary, and practical wisdom, concerned with what can be otherwise, separates the epistemology of science from that of action.
* **Major objections:**
  * The demonstrative model is too rigid for empirical sciences and does not describe actual scientific practice.
  * The account of *nous* is underdeveloped. Exactly how it grasps necessary first principles from experience is unclear.
  * Aristotle's restriction of *epistēmē* to necessary truths excludes most empirical knowledge of contingent facts, or relegates it to knowledge of what holds "for the most part."
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle produced the first systematic philosophy of science. It includes the axiomatic structure of sciences, the distinction between explanation and mere inference, the analysis of definition as explanatory, and an empiricist account of the origin of concepts and principles. Euclid's *Elements* realized something like the demonstrative ideal. Modern philosophy of explanation, theories of natural kinds, and debates about scientific understanding all engage with Aristotle's positions.

**Ethics and Human Flourishing**: `eudaimonism`, `virtue ethics`
* **Central thesis:** The highest human good is *eudaimonia*, living well and acting well. It consists in rational activity in accordance with virtue over a complete life. Virtues of character are stable dispositions to feel and choose in a "mean," acquired through habituation and guided by practical wisdom (*phronēsis*). The best human life, in a strict sense, is theoretical contemplation (*theōria*). The life of practical virtue is happy in a secondary sense.
* **The structure of ends (Nicomachean Ethics I.1–2, I.7):** Every action and pursuit aims at some good. Ends form hierarchies: bridle-making serves horsemanship, which serves generalship. If there is some end desired for its own sake, for the sake of which we desire everything else, that is the highest good. Everyone agrees to call it *eudaimonia*, but they disagree about what it is (I.4): pleasure, honor, wealth, or contemplation (I.5).
* **Formal criteria (I.7):** The highest good must be complete (*teleion*): chosen always for its own sake and never for the sake of something else. It must also be self-sufficient: by itself it makes life choiceworthy and lacking in nothing. *Eudaimonia* meets both criteria.
* **The function argument (I.7, 1097b22–1098a20):**
  1. The good of anything that has a function (*ergon*) lies in its performing that function well. A good flautist plays the flute well.
  2. Humans have a function, as their parts and the various crafts do.
  3. The human function is not mere life, which plants share, nor perception, which animals share, but the activity of the soul in accordance with reason (*logos*).
  4. A good human performs this function well, which is to say in accordance with virtue (*aretē*).
  5. Therefore the human good is "activity of soul in accordance with virtue, and if there are more virtues than one, in accordance with the best and most complete—and in a complete life."
* **Objections to the function argument:**
  * Does something's having a function establish what is good *for* it, as opposed to what makes it a good specimen?
  * Is the human function what is distinctive of humans, or what is characteristic of them? Distinctiveness would exclude perception and nutrition but would include, for instance, laughing or cruelty.
  * Does "in accordance with the best and most complete virtue" refer to a single virtue, contemplation, or to the complete set?
  
  Defenders (Korsgaard, Whiting, Lawrence) interpret "function" as the characteristic form of life, so that the argument links human nature to human good without the naturalistic fallacy G. E. Moore later identified.
* **External goods:** Happiness requires external goods: friends, wealth, political power, good birth, good children, and beauty (I.8). Great misfortunes such as Priam's can damage it (I.10). The virtuous person will never become miserable, since he will act finely in any circumstances. But he will not be blessedly happy if he meets Priam's fortunes. Aristotle thus takes a middle position between the Socratic or Stoic view that virtue is sufficient for happiness and the popular view that happiness depends on fortune.
* **Virtue of character (II):** Virtue is not by nature but arises from habituation (*ethos*). We become just by doing just acts, as we become builders by building (II.1). Fully virtuous action requires that the agent:
  * know what he is doing,
  * choose it, and choose it for its own sake,
  * act from a firm and unchanging state (II.4).
  
  Virtue is defined as "a state concerned with choice, lying in a mean relative to us, this being determined by reason, the reason by which the person of practical wisdom would determine it" (II.6, 1106b36–1107a2).
* **The doctrine of the mean:** Each virtue lies between two vices, one of excess and one of deficiency, in feelings and actions. Courage lies between rashness and cowardice; generosity between prodigality and stinginess. The mean is "relative to us." It is not arithmetical: six pounds of food may be too much for a beginner and too little for Milo the wrestler. To feel and act "at the right times, about the right things, toward the right people, for the right end, and in the right way" is the mean and the best.
  * *What the mean is not:* It is not a recommendation of moderation. The right response may be intense anger.
  * *Exceptions:* Some actions and feelings have no mean, such as adultery, murder, and spite (II.6, 1107a8–17).
  * *Criticisms:* Bernard Williams and J. O. Urmson questioned the doctrine's value. Some virtues, like justice, do not fit the triadic model well. And the doctrine gives no independent criterion beyond the judgment of the person of practical wisdom.
* **Practical wisdom (VI):** *Phronēsis* is "a true state involving reason, concerned with action regarding what is good and bad for a human being" (VI.5, 1140b4–6). It requires deliberative excellence, perception of particulars, and knowledge of the good. "Virtue makes the goal right, practical wisdom the things toward the goal" (VI.12, 1144a7–9).
  * *The ends debate:* Does practical wisdom grasp the end? Humeans read Aristotle as holding that ends are set by desire and reason only finds means. Others (Wiggins, McDowell) argue that deliberation about "things toward the end" includes specifying what the end is in concrete situations: what counts as acting justly here. On this view, practical wisdom involves a perceptual sensitivity to the salient features of situations.
  * *Natural versus full virtue (VI.13):* Natural virtue, an innate tendency to courage or temperance without wisdom, is like a strong body without sight that stumbles. Full virtue requires practical wisdom. Since practical wisdom is unified, having it implies having all virtues. This is the reciprocity of the virtues, Aristotle's version of the Socratic unity thesis.
* **Contemplation and the best life (X.6–8):** Happiness is activity in accordance with the highest virtue: the virtue of intellect, whose activity is theoretical contemplation. Contemplation is the most continuous, pleasant, self-sufficient, and leisurely activity, loved for its own sake. It is the activity most akin to the divine. Humans should "as far as possible make ourselves immortal" (X.7, 1177b33). The life of practical virtue is happy "in a secondary degree," because it is concerned with human affairs.
* **The inclusivism debate:**
  * *Inclusivist reading (J. L. Ackrill):* *Eudaimonia* in Book I is an inclusive good that contains all intrinsic goods, including virtuous action and contemplation.
  * *Dominant or intellectualist reading (Richard Kraut and others):* *Eudaimonia* is identical with the single best activity, contemplation, and Book X makes this explicit.
  
  The debate turns on how to read "complete" in I.7 and how to reconcile Books I and X.
* **The Eudemian Ethics:** The *Eudemian Ethics* shares three books with the *Nicomachean Ethics* (NE V–VII = EE IV–VI). Its final chapter offers a different ideal: *kalokagathia*, nobility-and-goodness, the complete virtue, with the contemplation and service of god as the standard for choosing natural goods (VIII.3). Its relation to the *Nicomachean Ethics*, including relative date and authenticity, has been debated since Anthony Kenny's 1978 study.
* **Major objections:**
  * The doctrine of the mean and the standard of the person of practical wisdom risk circularity and provide limited action-guidance.
  * The theory is elitist. It requires leisure, wealth, and a favored social position, and Aristotle denies virtue to slaves and women in the full sense.
  * The relation between practical and contemplative happiness may be unstable.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle's ethics is the classical source of virtue ethics. It articulates the eudaimonist framework, the role of habituation and character in moral development, the importance of practical judgment over rules, and the connection between moral and human nature. Elizabeth Anscombe's "Modern Moral Philosophy" (1958) and the subsequent work of Philippa Foot, Alasdair MacIntyre, Rosalind Hursthouse, and John McDowell revived Aristotelian ethics as an alternative to consequentialism and Kantian deontology.

**Moral Responsibility, Friendship, and Justice**
* **Central thesis:** Agents are responsible for their voluntary actions and, to an extent, for the characters from which those actions spring. Akrasia, acting against one's better judgment, is possible, but only through a specific failure in practical knowledge. Friendship (*philia*) is essential to the good life. Justice concerns the proper distribution and exchange of goods among members of a community. Equity (*epieikeia*) corrects the law where its generality leads it to err.
* **Voluntary action (Nicomachean Ethics III.1):** An action is voluntary (*hekousion*) when its origin is in the agent, who knows the particular circumstances of the action. An action is involuntary when done under force, where the origin is external and the agent contributes nothing, or through ignorance of particulars, when the agent later regrets it.
  * *Mixed actions:* Some actions are done from fear of greater evils, like throwing cargo overboard in a storm. They are voluntary at the moment of action, though in the abstract no one would choose them.
  * *Acting in ignorance versus by ignorance:* The drunk acts in ignorance, but his ignorance is his own fault. Ignorance of the universal—what one ought to do—is not exculpatory but a sign of vice.
* **Choice (prohairesis, III.2–3):** Choice is "deliberate desire of things in our power." It is narrower than the voluntary: children and animals act voluntarily but do not choose. Deliberation concerns not ends but "the things toward the ends." It is a search, like a geometrical analysis, from the end back to what can be done here and now.
* **Responsibility for character (III.5):** People are partly responsible for their character, since they became unjust by repeatedly doing unjust acts. Once they have become unjust, they cannot stop being so simply by wishing, "just as one who has thrown a stone cannot get it back." The argument that we are responsible for our ends and for our appearances of the good (1114a31–b25) is often read as a compatibilist account. Aristotle does not discuss determinism in the modern sense, so the label "compatibilism" is anachronistic. His concern is with conditions of praise, blame, and correction.
* **Akrasia (VII.1–10):**
  * *Method:* Aristotle states his method explicitly. Set out the appearances (*phainomena*), work through the puzzles, and preserve as many reputable opinions as possible (VII.1, 1145b2–7). Socrates' denial of akrasia contradicts the appearances, but there is something in it.
  * *Analysis (VII.3):* Aristotle distinguishes several ways of "having knowledge." One can have it without using it. One can know the universal premise without grasping the particular. And one can have knowledge "in a way," like someone asleep, mad, or drunk, or like actors reciting lines or learners repeating Empedocles' verses without understanding.
  * *The practical syllogism:* The akratic has a universal premise ("one should not taste sweets") and a particular one ("this is sweet"). Appetite, operating through a competing universal ("everything sweet is pleasant"), prevents the conclusion from being drawn and acted on. The akratic does not fully possess the last premise, the perceptual one. Hence "the knowledge present when akrasia occurs is not knowledge in the full sense, nor is it this knowledge that is dragged about by passion" (1147b15–17).
  * *Kinds of akrasia:* Impetuosity (*propeteia*) acts without deliberating. Weakness (*astheneia*) deliberates but fails to abide by the result.
  * *Interpretations:* Aristotle's account can be read as partly vindicating Socrates, by denying that full practical knowledge is overpowered. David Charles and others argue that Aristotle recognizes a genuine conflict between a reasoned judgment and appetite, which modern theories such as Donald Davidson's reformulate.
* **Friendship (VIII–IX):** *Philia* is reciprocated and recognized goodwill and affection. Its three forms correspond to three objects of love: the useful, the pleasant, and the good.
  * *Friendships of utility and pleasure:* These are incidental and easily dissolved, since the friend is loved not for who he is but for what he provides.
  * *Character friendship:* This holds between good people who love each other for their character. It is complete, stable, and rare. It also includes the benefits of the other forms.
  * *Another self:* The friend is "another self" (IX.4, 1166a31–32). The good person's relation to his friend reflects his relation to himself.
  * *Self-love (IX.8):* Self-love is good when it means loving the best part of oneself, the intellect, and gaining the fine. It is bad when it means grasping at money and pleasure.
* **Why the happy need friends (IX.9):** The good person, though self-sufficient, needs friends. Contemplating the actions of a good friend, which are like one's own, is pleasant. Shared activity makes virtuous activity more continuous. And living together, sharing discussion and thought, is what human life is. Aristotle also suggests that perceiving a friend's existence is choiceworthy just as perceiving one's own existence is.
* **Political friendship:** Friendship "holds cities together," and lawgivers care more for it than for justice (VIII.1). Political friendship, or concord (*homonoia*), is agreement about matters of common interest. *Philia* thus has a social dimension, connecting ethics and politics.
* **Justice (V):**
  * *General justice:* Lawfulness, complete virtue in relation to another person.
  * *Particular justice:* Opposed to *pleonexia*, grasping for more than one's share. It has two forms.
    * *Distributive justice:* Allocates honors, wealth, and other divisible goods according to merit (*axia*), by geometrical proportion: as person A is to person B, so A's share is to B's share. What counts as merit is disputed according to political constitution: freedom for democrats, wealth or good birth for oligarchs, virtue for aristocrats (V.3).
    * *Corrective justice:* Restores equality in transactions, voluntary (contracts) and involuntary (theft, assault). It follows arithmetical proportion, regardless of the parties' merit. The judge restores the mean between gain and loss.
  * *Reciprocity in exchange (V.5):* Money provides a common measure, grounded in need (*chreia*), making commensurable what is not naturally so.
  * *Natural and legal justice (V.7):* Political justice is partly natural, with the same force everywhere, and partly legal or conventional. Aristotle insists that natural justice can still vary. The example is the right hand, which is naturally stronger, though anyone can become ambidextrous.
* **Equity (V.10):** Law must speak universally, but some cases cannot be correctly covered by a universal rule. When the law errs by being too general, equity corrects it, deciding as the legislator would have if present. Aristotle compares it to the "Lesbian rule," a flexible lead ruler used to measure curved moldings. The equitable person is therefore better than the strictly just person, without being opposed to justice.
* **Major objections:**
  * Aristotle's theory of distributive justice depends on a contestable notion of merit that he does not resolve.
  * His account of responsibility for character may not answer worries about the influence of upbringing and luck.
  * His analysis of akrasia is ambiguous: does it ultimately concede the Socratic point?
  * Character friendship, being rare and restricted to the virtuous, makes friendship an elite good.
* **Philosophical significance:** Aristotle's distinctions between distributive and corrective justice and between justice and equity became foundational for Western legal and political thought, through Roman law, Aquinas, and the common law. His analysis of voluntariness informs criminal law (mens rea, duress, mistake). His account of friendship anticipated the modern ethics of partiality and personal relationships. His theory of akrasia remains a reference point in action theory.

**Political Philosophy**: `political naturalism`, `perfectionism`
* **Central thesis:** The polis exists by nature. It is the community in which human beings achieve self-sufficiency and the good life. Humans are by nature political animals. A constitution is just to the extent that it aims at the common good rather than the advantage of the rulers. The best constitution is the one in which citizens can live the best life. Practical politics must also consider constitutions that are best possible under given conditions.
* **The naturalness of the polis (Politics I.2):** The household arises to meet daily needs. The village unites households. The polis unites villages and achieves self-sufficiency. It "comes into being for the sake of living, but exists for the sake of living well" (1252b27–30). Because the earlier communities are natural, so is the polis, which is their end. "A human being is by nature a political animal" (1253a2–3). Humans alone have speech (*logos*), which indicates the advantageous and the harmful, the just and the unjust. Bees and herd animals have only voice, which expresses pleasure and pain (1253a7–18). Anyone who cannot live in a community, or does not need to because he is self-sufficient, "is either a beast or a god."
* **Priority of the polis:** The polis is prior by nature to the household and the individual, as the whole is prior to its parts. A hand severed from the body is a hand only homonymously (1253a19–29). This is the basis of the claim that the individual is not self-sufficient in isolation. Critics read it as an organicism that subordinates individuals to the state. Others argue that the priority claim is functional: individuals cannot realize their nature outside the polis. It is not a claim that the polis's good overrides theirs. Fred Miller argues that Aristotle's theory contains an implicit theory of rights grounded in justice.
* **Natural slavery (I.4–7):** Some humans are slaves by nature: they participate in reason enough to perceive it but not to possess it, and benefit from being ruled (1254b20–24). Aristotle acknowledges that some people are slaves only by law or convention, as captives of war. He admits that nature often fails to make the bodies of natural slaves distinctive. The argument is internally unstable. Aristotle cannot identify natural slaves with actual slaves. His premise that some people lack deliberative capacity is not supported by evidence. The tension with his general account of human beings as rational is evident.
* **Women and the household:** Women have a deliberative capacity, but it is "without authority" (*akuron*, I.13, 1260a13). Political rule over equals, by contrast with the master's rule over slaves and the husband's rule over his wife, involves ruling and being ruled in turn. Aristotle's account of household rule mirrors his political taxonomy.
* **Critique of Plato's Republic (II.1–5):** Aristotle argues that Plato's goal of extreme unity destroys the polis, which is by nature a plurality of different kinds of people. Communal property leads to neglect, since "what is common to the greatest number receives the least care" (1261b33–35). Communal families dilute affection and confuse the obligations of kinship. Private property with common use, guided by virtue and education, is better.
* **Citizenship (III.1–5):** A citizen is one who shares in judgment and office (*krisis kai archē*, 1275a22–23). Citizenship is therefore relative to constitution: the democratic citizen differs from the oligarchic one. The good citizen's virtue is relative to the constitution, whereas the good man's virtue is unconditional. The two coincide only in the best constitution, and even there only in the ruler (III.4). Aristotle excludes manual laborers from citizenship in the best city, because they lack the leisure for virtue (III.5; VII.9).
* **Classification of constitutions (III.6–8):** Constitutions are correct if they aim at the common advantage and deviant if they aim at the advantage of the rulers.
  * Rule of one: kingship (correct) and tyranny (deviant).
  * Rule of few: aristocracy (correct) and oligarchy (deviant).
  * Rule of many: polity (correct) and democracy (deviant).
  
  Oligarchy and democracy are really defined by wealth and poverty, not number (III.8).
* **The wisdom of the multitude (III.11):** Many people, though not individually excellent, may collectively judge better than a few good ones. A feast to which many contribute is better than one provided by a single host. Each brings a part of virtue and practical wisdom (1281a40–b10). Jeremy Waldron called this the "doctrine of the wisdom of the multitude." It is an early argument for the epistemic value of democratic deliberation, though Aristotle limits its scope.
* **Rule of law versus the best man (III.15–17):** Law is "reason without desire" (1287a32). A good human judgment is needed where law is silent or inapplicable. If one person surpasses all others in virtue, it would be unjust to treat him as an equal, and he should be king (*pambasileia*). The tension between rule of law and rule of the best echoes Plato's *Statesman*.
* **The middle constitution (IV.11):** In actual cities, the best practicable constitution is a polity: a mix of oligarchy and democracy with a strong middle class. The middle class is most ready to obey reason, least prone to faction, and acts as a balancing force between rich and poor. Aristotle's empirical studies, including the reported 158 constitutions collected by his school (of which the *Constitution of the Athenians* survives), inform these practical recommendations in Books IV–VI, including the causes of revolution (*stasis*) and how to preserve each constitution.
* **The best constitution and education (VII–VIII):** The best constitution is the one in which anyone can live the best life. This requires a citizenry with leisure for virtue and philosophy. Education must be public, since the end of the city is one (VIII.1). It includes music, valued not only for relaxation but for the cultivation of character and for leisure (*scholē*), which is the end of work.
* **Major objections:**
  * Natural slavery, the subordination of women, and the exclusion of laborers from citizenship are not incidental. They follow from the perfectionist requirement that citizens have leisure for virtue.
  * The priority of the polis can be read as subordinating individuals to political ends.
  * The best constitution is restricted to a small, homogeneous city.
* **What survives:** Aristotle's theory nevertheless offers several lasting contributions:
  * The distinction between correct and deviant constitutions by the criterion of common good.
  * The analysis of citizenship as participation.
  * The argument for mixed constitutions and a middle class.
  * The epistemic argument for collective judgment.
  * The insistence that politics aims at the good life, not merely at security or exchange.
